# Periodic quasiflats in hierarchically hyperbolic spaces

**arXiv ID**: 2608.01513v1
**Authors**: Pénélope Azuelos, Mark Hagen
**Published**: 2026-08-02
**Categories**: math.GR, math.GT, math.MG
**Comments**: 65 pages, 1 figure
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.01513v1

## Abstract

We prove a quasiflat closing theorem and a coarse flat torus theorem for hierarchically hyperbolic groups (HHGs). Namely, given an HHG $G$, we prove that $G$ is hyperbolic if and only if it contains no $\mathbb Z^2$ subgroups and, if $A\leq G$ is virtually $\mathbb Z^n$, then there is an $A$--invariant $n$--dimensional uniform quality quasiflat $F$ such that any two points in $F$ are joined by a uniform-quality hierarchy path lying in $F$. The later is a consequence of a more detailed theorem describing a ``coarse minset'' for $A$ in $G$, which has various applications, including an ascending chain condition for virtually abelian subgroups, hierarchical quasiconvexity of highest abelian subgroups, and some geometric control over normalisers, centralisers, and commensurators of abelian subgroups. We use this to rule out HHG structures for certain Coxeter groups on the basis of their affine subgroups, and to give a new proof that virtually solvable subgroups of HHGs are virtually abelian, which simplifies the original proof by avoiding Gromov's polynomial growth theorem.

Periodic quasiflats in hierarchically hyperbolic spaces

## Periodic quasiflats in hierarchically
hyperbolic spacesPénélope AzuelosSchool of Mathematics, University of Bristol, Bristol BS8 1UG, UKpenelope.azuelos@bristol.ac.ukandMark HagenSchool of Mathematics, University of Bristol, Bristol BS8 1UG, UKmarkfhagen@posteo.net

## Abstract.

We prove a quasiflat closing theorem and a coarse flat torus theorem for hierarchically hyperbolic groups (HHGs). Namely, given an HHGGG, we prove thatGGis hyperbolic if and only if it contains noℤ2\mathbb{Z}^{2}subgroups and, ifA≤GA\leq Gis virtuallyℤn\mathbb{Z}^{n}, then there is anAA–invariantnn–dimensional uniform quality quasiflatFFsuch that any two points inFFare joined by a uniform-quality hierarchy path lying inFF. The later is a consequence of a more detailed theorem describing a “coarse minset” forAAinGG, which has various applications, including an ascending chain condition for virtually abelian subgroups, hierarchical quasiconvexity of highest abelian subgroups, and some geometric control over normalisers, centralisers, and commensurators of abelian subgroups. We use this to rule out HHG structures for certain Coxeter groups on the basis of their affine subgroups, and to give a new proof that virtually solvable subgroups of HHGs are virtually abelian, which simplifies the original proof by avoiding Gromov’s polynomial growth theorem.

## 1.Introduction

A common feature among spaces with some form of (coarse) non-positive curvature is that virtually abelian subgroups of their isometry groups are often particularly well-behaved. Here are two classical illustrations of this phenomenon. First, assumeXXis a Gromov-hyperbolic space. IfAAis a virtually abelian group acting by semisimple isometries onXX, then there is either anAA–invariant, uniformly bounded subset ofXXor anAA-invariant uniform quality quasiline on whichAAacts cocompactly. In particular, if the action ofAAis proper, thenAAis virtually cyclic with undistorted orbits.
Second is the Flat Torus Theorem for CAT(0) spaces, which states (in part): ifXXis a CAT(0) space andAAis a virtually abelian group acting onXXby semisimple isometries then there is anAA–invariant closed, convex flatF⊆XF\subseteq Xon whichAAacts cocompactly. At this level of generality, this was proven in[BH99], generalising earlier results about Hadamard manifolds[GW71,LY72].

Among the CAT(0) spaces widely studied in group theory are CAT(0) cube complexes. Chepoi famously characterised CAT(0) cube complexes as those cubical complexes whose11–skeletons are median graphs, and, in fact, ifXXis a CAT(0) cube complex, then equipping each cube with theℓ1\ell_{1}metric yields a path metricddthat is median, and extends the median metric on the0–skeleton given by Chepoi’s theorem[Che00,Mie14]. A subspaceYYofXXthat is closed under taking medians is amedian subalgebra(the terminology reflects the correspondence between CAT(0) cube complexes and discrete median algebras[Rol98]). If the median subalgebraYYis a connected subcomplex, thenYYis itself a CAT(0) cube complex, and ispath-isometricin the sense that any two points inYYare joined by a geodesic ofXXlying inYY[HP22, Lem. 2.11].

Haglund showed in[Hag23b]that anyg∈Isom⁡(X,d)g\in\operatorname{Isom}(X,d)either fixes the barycentre of some cube, or has add–geodesic axisFF— i.e. a connected median subalgebra isometric toℝ\mathbb{R}— along which it acts as a translation111One must subdivide to arrange for there to be such an axis in the11–skeleton.. This can be viewed as a11–dimensional flat torus theorem for(X,d)(X,d), since the⟨g⟩\langle g\rangle–action onFFis cocompact.
The situation becomes more delicate for higher-rank (virtually) abelian subgroupsA≤Isom⁡(X,d)A\leq\operatorname{Isom}(X,d). On one hand, in[Woo17], Woodhouse generalised Haglund’s result by showing that ifAAis virtuallyℤn\mathbb{Z}^{n}, and theAA–action is proper, then (up to subdividing to remove inversions)AAstabilises a path-isometric subcomplexY⊆XY\subseteq X, whereYYis cubically isomorphic to∏i=1mCi\prod_{i=1}^{m}C_{i}form≥nm\geq nandC1,…,CmC_{1},\ldots,C_{m}a collection of CAT(0) cube complexes quasi-isometric toℝ\mathbb{R}. Note that Woodhouse’s result does not assert thatAAacts onYYcocompactly, and indeed it may be that no suchYYis cocompact. It is implicit in Woodhouse’s proof thatYYcan be taken to be a median subalgebra. In fact, Genevois elaborates on Woodhouse’s result in[Gen22], using the language of invariant median subalgebras. However, any theorem providing anAA–invariant median subalgebra must differ from the Flat Torus Theorem in one quite serious respect: one cannot in general expectAA–cocompactness.Figure 1.The(3,3,3)(3,3,3)Coxeter group acts on the standard tilingXXof𝔼3\mathbb{E}^{3}by unit cubes, preserving a22–dimensional flatFFthat crosses every hyperplane. The smallest median subalgebra ofXXcontainingFFis the whole ofXX.

Indeed, Figure1illustrates the case whereAAis the(3,3,3)(3,3,3)Coxeter group, andXXis the standard tiling of𝔼3\mathbb{E}^{3}by unit33–cubes; the action ofAAonXXcomes from applying Sageev’s construction to the set of walls in𝔼2\mathbb{E}^{2}provided by theAA–translates of the three reflection lines corresponding to the generators ofAA. Applying the CAT(0) flat torus theorem, and using the general fact that CAT(0)-convex subsets of a CAT(0) cube complex aredd–isometric (though not in generaldd–convex) yields anAA–cocompactdd–isometric subspaceF⊆XF\subseteq Xwhich, equipped with the CAT(0) metric, is isometric to𝔼2\mathbb{E}^{2}. Moreover, by perturbingFFequivariantly, one can make it into anAA–cocompact22–dimensionaldd–isometric subcomplexYY, homeomorphic toℝ2\mathbb{R}^{2}. However, as illustrated in Figure1, which showsFF, no median subalgebra ofXXis Hausdorff-close toFF.

Nonetheless, under reasonable conditions, one can findAA–cocompact bilipschitz flats in the median setting by relaxing the median subalgebra requirement, using insteaddd–isometricity. This is a consequence of the CAT(0) result, and the proof is short enough to present here, to motivate the subsequent discussions.

## Proposition\thethm.

Let(Q,d,μ)(Q,d,\mu)be a complete, connected, finite-rank median metric space and letA≤Isom⁡(Q)A\leq\operatorname{Isom}(Q)be a finitely generated virtually abelian subgroup acting properly, and letrrbe the rank ofAA. Then there is a proper cocompact action ofAAon𝔼r\mathbb{E}^{r}and anAA–equivariant bilipschitz embeddingf:𝔼r→Qf:\mathbb{E}^{r}\to Qsuch that any two points inf​(𝔼r)f(\mathbb{E}^{r})are joined by add–geodesic lying inf​(𝔼r)f(\mathbb{E}^{r}).

## Proof.

By[Bow16, Thm. 1.1, Thm. 8.3], there is a complete CAT(0) metricσ\sigmaonQQand a bilipschitz homeomorphismh:(Q,σ)→(Q,d)h:(Q,\sigma)\to(Q,d)that sends geodesics to reparameterised geodesics (as a map of sets,hhis just the identity). Moreover,Isom⁡(Q,d)\operatorname{Isom}(Q,d)acts on(Q,σ)(Q,\sigma)by isometries andhhisIsom⁡(Q,d)\operatorname{Isom}(Q,d)–equivariant. By, for instance,[Fio24, Cor. A], infinite order elements ofAAhave positive stable translation length on(Q,d)(Q,d), so sincehhis a bilipschitz map,AAacts on(Q,σ)(Q,\sigma)semisimply. Hence the Flat Torus Theorem[BH99, Cor. II.7.2]provides a cocompactAA–action on𝔼r\mathbb{E}^{r}and anAA–equivariant isometric embeddinge:𝔼r→(Q,σ)e:\mathbb{E}^{r}\to(Q,\sigma)withσ\sigma–convex image, and we conclude by takingf=h∘ef=h\circ e.
∎

## Remark\thethm.

We emphasise thatQQneed not be a locally compact space, and throughout this paper, we rarely make such an assumption. Hence, wherever we talk aboutpropermaps orproperactions, we are referring to metric properness.
∎

WhenQQis a CAT(0) cube complex, the mapfffrom Proposition1can be chosen so thatf​(𝔼r)f(\mathbb{E}^{r})is add–isometric subcomplex ofQQ. We view Proposition1as yet another reason to study such subcomplexes of CAT(0) cube complexes, which have recently received renewed attention in the very interesting paper[BCDK26]on so-calledamplesubsets ofQ(0)Q^{(0)}, which correspond to0–skeletons ofdd–isometric subcomplexes.222A particularly attractive feature of the notion is that it can be characterised in non-metric terms, providedQQis a finite-dimensional CAT(0) cube complex: the subcomplexY⊆QY\subseteq Qisdd–isometric if and only ifY∩h1∩⋯∩hkY\cap h_{1}\cap\cdots\cap h_{k}is empty or connected wheneverh1,…,hkh_{1},\ldots,h_{k}are hyperplanes ofQQ.In some sense, the present paper is about a “coarse-ification” ofdd–isometricity in the setting of hierarchically hyperbolic spaces.


Hierarchically hyperbolic spaces (HHSs) were introduced in[BHS17a,BHS19]to capture the similarities in the large-scale structure of mapping class groups and compact special groups. These spaces are, in essence, highly organised examples of coarse median spaces, in the sense of Bowditch[Bow13]. The class of HHSs includes all hyperbolic spaces and is closed under taking products and under relative hyperbolicity (i.e. ifXXis hyperbolic relative to HHS subspaces thenXXis an HHS)[BHS19, Prop. 8.27, Thm. 9.3], but one can weaken the bounded penetration property of relative hyperbolicity to allow for the peripheral subspaces to interact in a coarse product structure, allowing the class to accommodate, for example, all right-angled Artin groups. See[BHS19]for the formal definition in its modern form.

The fact that HHSs are coarse median spaces is witnessed by the (strictly stronger) cubical approximation theorem[BHS21, Thm. 2.1], which states: for anynnpoints{x1,…,xn}\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}in an HHS, there is a coarsely median-preserving quasi-isometric embedding, with constants depending only on the HHS structure andnn, from a CAT(0) cube complex to the hull (see Subsection2.8) of{x1,…,xn}\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}, such that eachxix_{i}is in the image of the 0-skeleton. In a large subclass of HHSs, this local property becomes global: Petyt showed that any colourable hierarchically hyperbolic group (HHG) admits a quasi-isometry to a CAT(0) cube complex[Pet26].

Geodesics in HHSs are not in general closely related to the HHS structure.
Instead, one usually works with hierarchy paths, which are quasigeodesics that do respect the HHS structure (see Definition2.6).
Hierarchical quasiconvexity is defined in terms of these paths: a subspaceYYof an HHSXXis hierarchically quasiconvex (HQC) if every hierarchy path between points inYYis close toYY(of course, this should be quantified appropriately; see Definition2.6).
Hierarchy paths are analogues ofℓ1\ell^{1}-geodesics in CAT(0) cube complexes, so hierarchical quasiconvexity is the analogue of median convexity in CAT(0) cube complexes. In light of the preceding discussion, a flat torus theorem for HHSs will need to involve a notion of convexity which imitates CAT(0) convexity, orℓ1\ell_{1}–metric isometricity, instead.

For this purpose, we introduce the following notion: a subspaceYYof an HHSXXisexistentially hierarchically quasiconvex (EHQC)if, for allx,y∈Yx,y\in Ythere is a hierarchy path in a uniform neighbourhood ofYYwhich joinsxxtoyy(again, this should be parametrised correctly; see Definition2.7).
As in the results about CAT(0) and hyperbolic spaces, we also need a semisimplicity assumption, provided by Definition5.

A simplified version of our “HHS flat torus theorem” is the following, which answers the special cases of Questions 6.1 and 6.2 in[AHPZ25]concerning HHGs:{thm}

[Theorem6.(2)]
Letn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}and letAAbe a virtuallyℤn\mathbb{Z}^{n}group. SupposeAAis a subgroup of an HHG(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})— or, more generally, thatAAacts properly and hierarchically semisimply by HHS automorphisms on an HHS(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}). Then there is a proper cocompact action ofAAon the Euclidean space𝔼n\mathbb{E}^{n}, anAA–invariant EHQC subspaceF⊆XF\subseteq X, and anAA–equivariant quasi-isometryF→𝔼nF\rightarrow\mathbb{E}^{n}. Moreover, the EHQC and quasi-isometry constants depend only onXX.

We prove a more detailed statement in Theorem6. In particular, we describe the coarse structure of the union of a natural family ofAA-invariant, uniform quality EHQC quasiflats, proving that this subspace isAA-equivariantly quasi-isometric to a productY×C×𝔼nY\times C\times\mathbb{E}^{n}, whereYYis an injective space with a trivialAA-action andCCis a CAT(0) space with a trivialAA-action. In the case whereXXis the Cayley graph of an HHGGGandA≤GA\leq G, this subspace can be taken to be the normaliser ofAA. Without the hierarchical semisimplicity assumption, the theorem fails, as is already evident in the case whereXXis hyperbolic andAAis generated by a single parabolic isometry.

In[FFMS25, Thm. 3.15], Fournier-Facio, Mangioni and Sisto prove that, ifGGis a hierarchically hyperbolic group (HHG),KKis finitely generated andK↪G↠EK\hookrightarrow G\twoheadrightarrow Eis a central extension, then the corresponding element ofH2​(E,K)H^{2}(E,K)is represented by a bounded cocycle. We generalise this to the setting of hierarchically semisimple actions on HHSs (in particular, the following theorem applies to all subgroups of HHGs).\getkeytheorem

central extensions

Gersten showed[Ger92]that ifK↪G↠EK\hookrightarrow G\twoheadrightarrow Eis a central extension represented by a bounded cocycle, thenGGis naturally quasi-isometric to the direct productK×EK\times E, but the converse to this false in general[FS23], even for CAT(0) groups[AM24, Thm. 1.1].

## Remark\thethm(Virtual direct products).

The CAT(0) Flat Torus Theorem includes a stronger version of Theorem6in the free abelian case: supposeBBis a finitely generated group acting by isometries on a CAT(0) spaceXXand supposeA⊴BA\unlhd Bis a normal subgroup whose action onXXis proper and by semisimple isometries. Then there is a finite index subgroupB′≤BB^{\prime}\leq Bwhich containsAAas a direct factor[BH99, Thm. II.7.1.(5)].

The corresponding statement for actions on HHSs cannot hold, as we now illustrate using mapping class groups. LetΣ\Sigmabe a connected orientable surface of finite type, and suppose thatΣ\Sigmais either closed with genus≥3\geq 3, or has genus≥2\geq 2and at least one boundary component or two punctures. Then the mapping class groupℳ​𝒞​𝒢​(Σ)\mathcal{MCG}(\Sigma)contains finitely generated subgroups that split as central extensions but are not virtually direct products (see[BH99, Thm. II.7.26]). Since mapping class groups are HHGs, and in particular act properly and hierarchically semisimply by HHS automorphisms on HHSs, this implies that Theorem6is optimal in the sense that the conclusion cannot be improved to give virtually split extensions as in the CAT(0) case.
∎

As in the CAT(0) case, our version of the flat torus theorem has many applications. To begin with, HHGs (in fact, a more general class of groups) satisfy the ascending chain condition for virtually abelian subgroups:\getkeytheorem

cor:ascending chains

Givenn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}, a virtuallyℤn\mathbb{Z}^{n}subgroupA≤GA\leq Gof a groupGGishighestif no finite index subgroup ofAAis contained in a subgroup ofGGisomorphic toℤn+1\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}.

In[DHS17]it is stated that, ifGGis a hierarchically hyperbolic group, then anyH≤GH\leq Geither containsF2F_{2}or is virtually abelian; the proof given in[DHS17]had a gap which is corrected in[DHS20]assuming finite generation ofHH; Corollary7allows one to remove the finite generation hypothesis (apply the result from[DHS20]to finitely generated subgroupsHn≤H≤GH_{n}\leq H\leq Gwith⋃nHn=H\bigcup_{n}H_{n}=H). Hence the version of the Tits alternative stated in[DHS17, Thm. 9.15]holds as stated.

Relatedly, we give a new proof that virtually solvable subgroups of HHGs are virtually abelian which, unlike that in[DHS17,DHS20], avoids Gromov’s polynomial growth theorem:\getkeytheorem

cor:solvable

Recall that, in the CAT(0) cubical setting, abelian subgroups of cubulated groups may not be convex-cocompact, but Wise–Woodhouse showed[WW17]that, ifGGacts geometrically on a CAT(0) cube complexXX, andA≤GA\leq Gis a highest virtually abelian subgroup, then there is a convex,AA–cocompact subcomplexY⊆XY\subseteq X. In exact analogy, we show:\getkeytheorem

cor:highest-abelian

In the important case whereGGis the mapping class group of a finite-type hyperbolic surface, abelian subgroups were well-understood even before the introduction of curve graphs, subsurface projections, and the “hierarchical” viewpoint. For example, Corollary7, and the upper bound on rank for free abelian subgroups, was established in[BLM83]using Thurston’s classification of mapping classes, systems of reducing curves, etc. Although the notions of HQC and EHQC subspaces/subgroups cannot be defined without first introducing some “hierarchical” ideas — enough to be able to talk about coarse medians, for instance — the extra input of Thurston theory seems to simplify proofs like that of, say, Corollary7.2in the mapping class group case. However, as pointed out to us by Jason Behrstock, our results recover a useful information about virtually abelian subgroups in the mapping class group case without recourse to the powerful Thurston classification.333There is a limited analogue of the Thurston classification for general HHGs, which does play an important role in this paper, that goes back to the classification of HHS automorphisms/“coarse rank rigidity” in[DHS17]and has its most modern form in[PS23, Thm. 5.1]. However, this is a statement about an action on a hierarchical structure, and doesn’t rely on anything specific to surfaces, or even directly imply the Thurston classification in the MCG case.

Corollary7.2relies on a stronger result, Corollary7.1, showing that the normaliserNG​(A)N_{G}(A)is EHQC inGGand that there is a finite-index subgroupA1≤AA_{1}\leq Awhose centraliserCG​(A1)C_{G}(A_{1})is hierarchically quasiconvex (which, recall, is stronger than EHQC). Corollary7.2combines with results in[HHP23,Hod23]to limit the possible point groups of highest virtually abelian subgroups of HHGs, which we make precise in Corollary7.2. In Corollary7.2, we apply this to Coxeter groups; specifically, we rule out HHG structures on Coxeter groups containing certain “poison” affine subgroups. We suspect that Coxeter groups without “poison” affine subgroups are HHGs, and that this is closely related to the question of cocompact cubulation. It is not currently known exactly which Coxeter groups are cocompactly cubulated, although progress on a characterisation is provided by[NR03,Wil98,FLS24,HW10]. In view of Remark7.2, Corollary7.2, and existing results, we conjecture the following.

## Conjecture\thethm.

A Coxeter group is an HHG if and only if it is cocompactly cubulated.

Corollary7.1and Lemma2.6combine to yield another interesting consequence:{cor}

Let(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S})be a hierarchically hyperbolic group. Then there existsN<∞N<\infty, depending on the groupGGbut not the HHG structure, such that the following holds. LetA≤GA\leq Gbe a virtually abelian subgroup. Then there is a free abelian subgroupA1≤AA_{1}\leq Asuch that[A:A1]≤N[A:A_{1}]\leq NandCG​(A1)C_{G}(A_{1})is hierarchically quasiconvex with respect to any HHG structure onGG, and in particular,CG​(A1)C_{G}(A_{1})admits an HHS structure where the left multiplication action ofCG​(A1)C_{G}(A_{1})on itself is an action by HHS automorphisms.

Motivation for Corollary1comes from the fact that a given finitely generated subgroupH≤GH\leq Gmay by HQC or not, depending on how we choose the HHG structure, which, in general, can be done in many ways; see[Man24]and also[AHPZ25, Thm. 5.15, Rem. 5.16].

Passing to the finite-index subgroupA1≤AA_{1}\leq Ain Corollary1is needed in order to rule out the possibility thata​V⊥VaV\bot Vfor certainV∈𝔖V\in\mathfrak{S}, wherea∈Aa\in A. In particular, if(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S})has the global property that elements of𝔖\mathfrak{S}are not orthogonal to theirGG–translates, then we get hierarchical quasiconvexity of centralisers. An emblematic example of such a situation is whenGGis the fundamental group of a compact special cube complex, with the HHG structure from[BHS17a].{cor}

Let(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S})be a hierarchically hyperbolic group and suppose thatg​U⊬UgU\>\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\nvdash$}}Ufor allg∈Gg\in GandU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}. LetAAbe a free abelian subgroup. ThenCG​(A)C_{G}(A)is hierarchically quasiconvex.

This is proved in Section7, as part of the proof of Corollary7.1. The hypothesis that elements of𝔖\mathfrak{S}are not orthogonal to theirGG–translates holds in a finite-index subgroup wheneverGGiscolourablein the sense of[DMS23]; while there are non-colourable HHGs[Hag23a], many of the natural examples of HHGs, like mapping class groups[BBF15]and compact special groups[BHMS24a], are colourable.

## Remark\thethm.

The preceding corollary is related to work of Genevois[Gen22, Thm. 5.1, Lem. 5.2], which shows that, wheneverGGacts geometrically on a cube complexXX, then, up to subdividingXXonce, for each infinite orderg∈Gg\in G, there is anℓ1\ell_{1}–isometric subcomplexM​i​n​(g)Min(g)ofXX— a connected median subalgebra — stabilised cocompactly byCG​(⟨g⟩)C_{G}(\langle g\rangle). Now, ifGGalso acts cospecially, then, as shown in[Gen21],M​i​n​(g)Min(g)coincides with thestableminsetS​M​i​n​(g)SMin(g)which, by[Gen21, Rem. 3.7], is convex, giving cubical convexity (and hence hierarchical quasiconvexity) of centralisers. See also[HP20, Prop. 9.6].
∎

We finally discuss the structure of commensurators of virtually abelian subgroups in an HHG(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S}), inspired by the corresponding discussion of commensurators of abelian subgroups of CAT(0) groups from[HP20]. We prove:\getkeytheorem

cor:commensurators

An observation in[HP20]about complete square complexes (CSCs) shows that this result is sharp, in that one cannot in general replace the finitely generated subgroupKKwith the entire commensuratorCommG⁡(A)\operatorname{Comm}_{G}({A}). Now, in[HP20, Cor. 5.10], Huang–Prytuła show that, ifGGis compact special (in particular, both a CAT(0) group and an HHG),CommG⁡(A)\operatorname{Comm}_{G}({A})is finitely generated and coincides withNG​(A′)N_{G}(A^{\prime})for some finite-indexA′≤AA^{\prime}\leq A.

WhenGGis a mapping class group,[JRLASSn24, Thm. 1.1, Rem. 1.2]shows that, ifA≤GA\leq Gis a virtually abelian subgroup, thenCommG⁡(A)\operatorname{Comm}_{G}({A})coincides withNG​(A′)N_{G}(A^{\prime}), whereA′A^{\prime}is a finite-index withAA. This follows from Corollary7.1once one knows thatCommG⁡(A)\operatorname{Comm}_{G}({A})is finitely generated, which is shown for mapping class groups in Corollary 4.6 in loc. cit., but which does not hold for general HHGs.

In various recent papers, some attention is given to the possibility of a theory of “algebraically well-behaved” HHGs, encompassing compact special groups and mapping class groups. Roughly speaking, such a theory should involve imposing conditions ensuring that various HQC subgroups are separable, and that coarse products of subgroups must arise from a certain amount of commutation. For instance, the idea of requiring separability of product region subgroups, motivated by mapping class groups[LM07], is explored in[HP22, Prop. 3.2]. The correct commutation condition may turn out to vary according to the exact applications one wants, but the notions of “weakly commutative”[OP25, Defn. 3.4], “decomposable”[DMS25]or “algebraic”[CRHK24]HHGs, or the conditions from[AB23]are all candidate conditions aimed at excluding colourable HHGs whose algebraic properties do not reflect their geometry in straightforward ways, like irreducible CSC groups. We wonder if imposing the condition that abelian subgroups have finitely generated commensurators is a useful move in this direction.

## 1.1.Quasiflat closing

The famous Flat Closing Problem for CAT(0) spaces asked whether a groupGGacting geometrically on a CAT(0) spaceXXwith an isometrically embedded𝔼2\mathbb{E}^{2}must contain aℤ2\mathbb{Z}^{2}subgroup. This was spectacularly resolved by the counterexample provided by Martelli in the recent paper[Mar25].

In contrast, we prove the following positive result for hierarchically hyperbolic groups:\getkeytheorem

higher-rank

A special case of this, Theorem7.3, says that any HHG is either hyperbolic or contains aℤ2\mathbb{Z}^{2}subgroup. In Section7.3, we first present a proof of the latter theorem, then generalise the argument to prove Theorem7.3.

For CAT(0) cube complexes, Theorem7.3and the results in[BHS17a]immediately yield:{cor}

Flat closing holds for CAT(0) cube complexes which admit a factor system.

It is unknown whether flat closing holds for general cocompactly cubulated groups (not all of which admit factor systems[She23]), but there were some previously known positive results. For example, it is well known that ifGGis virtually compact special in the sense of[HW08], thenGGis hyperbolic or containsℤ2\mathbb{Z}^{2}. Work of Sageev–Wise[SW11]yields the same conclusion ifGGacts geometrically on a CAT(0) cube complex satisfying a condition that limits intersections between certain sets of hyperplane stabilisers (and is therefore somewhat similar in spirit to the existence of a factor system).

Theorem7.3follows from Corollary7.2together with an argument that is elementary, in the sense that it uses only the core concepts of the theory of HHSes. The key observation is that Corollary7.2reduces the problem to that of finding someg∈Gg\in Gsuch that there are at least two distinctU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}for whichπU​(⟨g⟩)\pi_{U}(\langle g\rangle)is unbounded. In the case of a cube complex with a factor system, this can also be deduced from[WW17]. The factor system case also follows by combining[CS11, Cor. D]and[HS20, Prop. 2.7]; however, it appears that this statement does not appear elsewhere in the literature.

## 1.2.Sketch of the proof

Here is a summary of the proof of Theorem1.

As in the proof of[HRSS25, Prop. 2.17], we first find pairwise-orthogonalU1,…,Un∈𝔖U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}such that{U1,…,Un}\{U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}\}isAA–invariant, and anyW∈𝔖W\in\mathfrak{S}withπW\pi_{W}unbounded onAA–orbits must satisfyW⊑UiW\sqsubseteq U_{i}for someii, using[PS23, Thm. 5.1].
This allows us to use tools from[BHS19], like the realisation theorem and distance formula, along with a modified version of the “factored space” construction from[BHS17b], to reduce to the case whereX=H×XˇX=H\times\widecheck{X}(coarsely), whereHHis an HHS whose index set contains the set𝔘\mathfrak{U}of allUUnested in someUiU_{i}, andXˇ\widecheck{X}is an HHS whereAAacts by HHS automorphisms with bounded orbits. Moreover, lettingA′≤AA^{\prime}\leq Abe a bounded-index subgroup fixing eachUiU_{i}, we observe that𝒞​Ui\mathcal{C}U_{i}contains anA′A^{\prime}–invariant uniform quasilineγUi\gamma_{U_{i}}. In fact, the subgroupA′A^{\prime}stabilises two pointsp±p^{\pm}in the HHS boundary from[DHS17], andγUi\gamma_{U_{i}}joins the two pointspUi±∈∂𝒞​Uip^{\pm}_{U_{i}}\in{\partial}\mathcal{C}U_{i}to whichp±p^{\pm}project.

ForU∈𝔘U\in\mathfrak{U}, eitherπU​(X)\pi_{U}(X)is uniformly bounded, orγUi\gamma_{U_{i}}projects to a (bounded, but not uniformly) uniform quasigeodesicγU\gamma_{U}. By passing to a suitable uniformly HQC subspace, we can assume thatπU​(H)\pi_{U}(H)is either uniformly bounded, or uniformly coarsely coincides withγU\gamma_{U}, for allUU. These arguments are collected in Proposition5.

In Proposition4, we use the fact thatXˇ\widecheck{X}is coarsely dense in its injective hull, as shown in[HHP23], together with Lang’s fixed-point theorem for injective spaces[Lan13]and the fact thatAAhas bounded orbits inXˇ\widecheck{X}, to choose a pointxˇ∈Xˇ\check{x}\in\widecheck{X}whoseAA–orbit has uniformly bounded diameter. This reduces the problem to finding the requiredAA–invariant, uniformly EHQC, uniform quasiflat inHH.

This is done using Theorem3, which is a variant of Durham’s cubical model theorem, and in fact we borrow our strategy from[Dur23]. The latter is itself a considerable generalisation and strengthening of the cubical approximation theorem from[BHS21](which was reproved by Bowditch by different means in[Bow18a]), and strengthens Durham–Zalloum’s cubical model theorem for HHS boundary points[DZ22]. (See[Zal23]for a survey of the various cubical modelling tools available in HHSs, and[DMS23]for another strengthened form of cubical approximation for colourable HHGs.)

Given the inputs to Theorem3, Durham’s theorem would provide (depending in principle on the choicez0∈Hz_{0}\in Hof basepoint) a uniform quasi-isometry from a CAT(0) cube complexQQto the HHSHH, preserving (coarse) medians up to uniformly bounded error. Durham also obtains a form of equivariance, but it is not quite what we need. We restrict ourselves to a more limited setting than in Durham’s work, because we are concerned only with the case whereHHis the hull of a pair of boundary points (Durham allows arbitrary finite sets), which lets us dispense with basepoints and obtain a complete, connected, finite-rank median spaceQQand a uniformly quasi-median quasi-isometryH→QH\to Q, as in Durham’s theorem, but with the added feature thatAAacts onQQby isometries and the map toQQisAA–equivariant. From there, a result of Bowditch[Bow16]yields a compatible CAT(0) metric onQQto which we can apply the classical flat torus theorem to obtain the desired EHQC quasiflat inHH.

To prove the more detailed version of our theorem (Theorem6), we consider the set of allAA–invariant quasi-flats obtained in this way. The resulting quasi-product structureY×C×𝔼nY\times C\times\mathbb{E}^{n}comes from the fact that the fixed point set ofAAin the injective hull ofXˇ\widecheck{X}is itself injective, and that the CAT(0) flat torus theorem provides anAA–invariant subspaceC×𝔼n⊆QC\times\mathbb{E}^{n}\subseteq Qsuch thatCCis closed and convex (and thus CAT(0)).

## 1.3.Outline of the paper

Section2contains background on hierarchically hyperbolic spaces, as well as a few miscellaneous facts needed later in the paper. Section3is devoted to the proof of Theorem3, on median models of hulls. Section4deals with bounded actions on HHSs, yielding Proposition4. In Section5, we prove Proposition5, providing the necessary canonicalAA–invariant HQC and EHQC subspaces ofXX. In Section6, we combine these ingredients to prove our main theorems, Theorem6and Theorem6, and we apply these to obtain the various corollaries in Section7.

There is also AppendixA, where we restate several existing results about hierarchical hyperbolicity, making explicit how constants produced by these statements depend on the inputs. In no such case do we present a new proof; we just extract from the proofs in the literature the required information about constants. Typically, we require some output constant to depend only on the parameters of the HHS, and obtaining this is a matter of tracing through an existing proof to get a quantitative version of the statement we need.

We also thank Jason Behrstock, Giorgio Mangioni, Harry Petyt, and Abdul Zalloum for several useful and interesting comments on a previous version. MH is grateful to Carolyn Abbott, Harry Petyt, and Abdul Zalloum for discussions during the writing of[AHPZ25]leading to some of the questions addressed in this paper. Finally, we are particularly grateful to Jason Behrstock for some useful perspective on the mapping class group case, and for asking us a question that prompted us to work out the quasiflat closing theorem.

## 2.Background on hierarchically hyperbolic spaces

Fix a hierarchically hyperbolic space (HHS)(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}), as defined in[BHS19, Defn. 1.1]. We adopt the notation from there, some of which we recall here.

For eachU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}, letπU:X→𝒞​U\pi_{U}:X\to\mathcal{C}Ube the associated projection to the hyperbolic space𝒞​U\mathcal{C}U. WheneverU,V∈𝒞​UU,V\in\mathcal{C}UsatisfyU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq VorU⋔VU\pitchfork V, the uniformly bounded set in𝒞​V\mathcal{C}Vassociated toUUis denotedρVU\rho^{U}_{V}, and similarly, ifU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq V, there is a mapρUV:𝒞​V→𝒞​U\rho^{V}_{U}:\mathcal{C}V\to\mathcal{C}U. Letddbe the metric onXX, which is assumed to be a quasigeodesic space.
For eachV∈𝔖V\in\mathfrak{S}, letdVd_{V}be the (hyperbolic geodesic) metric on𝒞​U\mathcal{C}U. Following the usual convention, givenx,y∈Xx,y\in X, we writedV​(x,y)d_{V}(x,y)to meandV​(πV​(x),πV​(y))d_{V}(\pi_{V}(x),\pi_{V}(y)).

## 2.1.HHS boundary and boundary point projections

We recall the definition of underlying set of theHHS boundaryof(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})from[DHS17]. We will not need the topology.

## Definition\thethm.

TheHHS boundaryof an HHS(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})is a set∂X\partial Xof pointsλ∈∂X\lambda\in\partial X, each of which is determined by the following data:
- (1)

a pairwise orthogonal set of domainssupp⁡(λ)⊆𝔖\operatorname{supp}(\lambda)\subseteq\mathfrak{S}, called thesupportofλ\lambda;
- (2)

a point in the Gromov boundaryλU∈∂𝒞​U\lambda^{U}\in\partial\mathcal{C}Ufor eachU∈supp⁡(λ)U\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda);
- (3)

a collection of positive real numbers{aU:U∈supp⁡(λ)}\{a_{U}:U\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda)\}such that∑U∈supp⁡(λ)au=1\sum_{U\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda)}a_{u}=1.

Setsupp⟂(λ)≔{V∈𝔖:V⟂U\operatorname{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)\coloneqq\{V\in\mathfrak{S}:V\perp Ufor allU∈supp(λ)}U\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda)\}.
∎

## Notation\thethm.

Givenδ>0\delta>0, denote byMorseδ\textup{{Morse}}_{\delta}the function provided by the Morse lemma forδ\delta–hyperbolic geodesic spaces, i.e. for anyϵ1,ϵ2\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}, any(ϵ1,ϵ2)(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})–quasigeodesic in such a space is at Hausdorff distance at mostMorseδ​(ϵ1,ϵ2)\textup{{Morse}}_{\delta}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})from the geodesic joining its endpoints.
∎

The projection mapsπU\pi_{U}can be extended to the boundary as follows. This process is canonical unlessU∈supp⟂U\in\operatorname{supp}^{\perp}, in which case it requires a choice of basepoint inXX.

Letδ≥0\delta\geq 0be the hyperbolicity constant (so𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uisδ\delta-hyperbolic for allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}) and letℰ\mathcal{E}be the constant from the bounded geodesic image axiom ([BHS19, Defn. 1.1.(7)]). Letλ∈∂X\lambda\in\partial X, letU∈supp⁡(λ)U\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda), and letV∈𝔘V\in\mathfrak{U}be such thatV⋤UV\sqsubsetneq U. Denote byλUV⊆𝒞​U\lambda^{V}_{U}\subseteq\mathcal{C}Uthe union of all(1,20​δ)(1,20\delta)-quasi-geodesic raysγ\gammain𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uin the equivalence class corresponding to the boundary pointλU\lambda^{U}such thatγ∩𝒩ℰ+Morseδ​(1,20​δ)𝒞​U​(ρUV)=∅\gamma\cap\mathcal{N}^{\mathcal{C}U}_{\mathcal{E}+\textup{{Morse}}_{\delta}(1,20\delta)}(\rho^{V}_{U})=\emptyset.

## Definition\thethm(Projections of boundary points).

Letλ∈∂X\lambda\in\partial Xand letx0∈Xx_{0}\in X. For eachU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}, define theprojection based atx0x_{0}, denotedπU,x0​(λ)∈2𝒞​U∪∂𝒞​U\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda)\in 2^{\mathcal{C}U}\cup\partial\mathcal{C}U, as follows:
- •

ifU∈supp⟂⁡(λ)U\in\operatorname{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda)then letπU,x0​(λ)≔πU​(x0)\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\pi_{U}(x_{0});
- •

ifU∈supp⁡(λ)U\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda)then letπU,x0​(λ)≔λU\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\lambda^{U};
- •

ifU∉supp⁡(λ)U\notin\operatorname{supp}(\lambda)andU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq Vfor someV∈supp⁡(λ)V\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda)then letπU,x0​(λ)≔ρUV​(λVU)\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda^{U}_{V}). Also setρUV​(πV​(λ,x0))≔πU​(λ,x0)\rho^{V}_{U}(\pi_{V}(\lambda,x_{0}))\coloneqq\pi_{U}(\lambda,x_{0}).
- •

If none of the above hold, then the set𝒱≔{V∈supp(λ):V⋤U\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\{V\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda):V\sqsubsetneq UorV⋔U}V\pitchfork U\}is non-empty and we defineπU,x0​(λ)≔⋃V∈𝒱ρUV\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\rho^{V}_{U}.

IfV∈𝔖−supp⟂⁡(λ)V\in\mathfrak{S}-\operatorname{supp}^{\perp}(\lambda), thenπV,x0​(λ)\pi_{V,x_{0}}(\lambda)is independent ofx0x_{0}, so we can writeπV​(λ)≔πV,x0​(λ)\pi_{V}(\lambda)\coloneqq\pi_{V,x_{0}}(\lambda).
∎

## 2.2.HHS parameters

Our main result asserts that certain “output” quantities depend only on the quantities in the definition of an HHS ([BHS19, Defn. 1.1]), namely:
- (1)

qqis the quasigeodesic constant forXX, i.e. any two points are joined by a(q,q)(q,q)–quasigeodesic.
- (2)

δ\deltais the hyperbolicity constant, i.e.𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uisδ\delta–hyperbolic for allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}.
- (3)

ConstantsKKandξ\xi: for allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}, the coarse mapπU\pi_{U}is(K,K)(K,K)–coarsely lipschitz and hasKK–quasiconvex image, whileπU​(x)\pi_{U}(x)has diameter at mostξ\xifor allx∈Xx\in X. Also,diam⁡(ρVU)≤ξ\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\rho^{U}_{V})\leq\xiwheneverU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq VorU⋔VU\pitchfork V.
- (4)

There is a constantκ0\kappa_{0}such that all of the inequalities in[BHS19, Defn. 1.1.(4)](the transversality/consistency axiom) use the upper boundκ0\kappa_{0}.
- (5)

Thecomplexityχ∈ℕ\chi\in\mathbb{N}is the maximum length of a⋤\sqsubsetneq–chain in𝔖\mathfrak{S}.
- (6)

The large link axiom[BHS19, Defn. 1.1.(6)], bounded geodesic image axiom[BHS19, Defn. 1.1.(7)]and partial realisation axiom ([BHS19, Defn. 1.1.(8)]) each introduce one additional constant, denotedλ,ℰ\lambda,\mathcal{E}andα\alpharespectively.
- (7)

The uniqueness axiom ([BHS19, Defn. 1.1.(9)]) provides a functionθu:ℝ≥0→ℝ≥0\theta_{u}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}such thatd​(x,y)≤θu​(supV∈𝔖dV​(x,y))d(x,y)\leq\theta_{u}(\sup_{V\in\mathfrak{S}}d_{V}(x,y))for allx,y∈Xx,y\in X.

Using the definition of boundary projections in Section2.1, it is straightforward to prove:

## Lemma\thethm.

Ifλ∈∂X\lambda\in\partial X,x0∈Xx_{0}\in XandU∈𝔖−supp⁡(λ)U\in\mathfrak{S}-\operatorname{supp}(\lambda), thendiam⁡(πU,x0​(λ))≤ξ′\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda))\leq\xi^{\prime}, whereξ′\xi^{\prime}is a function ofξ\xi,α\alphaandℰ\mathcal{E}.

## Proof.

LetU∈𝔖−supp⁡(λ)U\in\mathfrak{S}-\operatorname{supp}(\lambda). There are three cases.

First suppose thatU⊥VU\bot Vfor allV∈supp⁡(λ)V\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda). Thendiam⁡(πU,x0​(λ))=diam⁡(πU​(x0))≤ξ\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda))=\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\pi_{U}(x_{0}))\leq\xi.

Second, suppose thatU⋔VU\pitchfork VorV⋤UV\sqsubsetneq Ufor someV∈supp⁡(λ)V\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda), i.e. the set𝒱\mathcal{V}from Definition2.1is nonempty. By definition,πU,x0​(λ)=⋃V∈𝒱ρUV\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda)=\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\rho^{V}_{U}. SinceV⊥V′V\bot V^{\prime}for all distinctV,V′∈𝒱V,V^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}, we havedU​(ρUV,ρUV′)≤2​αd_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U},\rho^{V^{\prime}}_{U})\leq 2\alpha, sodiam⁡(πU,x0​(λ))≤2​(α+ξ)\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda))\leq 2(\alpha+\xi).

Third, suppose thatU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq Vfor someV∈supp⁡(λ)V\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda). Recall thatλVU⊆𝒞​V\lambda^{U}_{V}\subseteq\mathcal{C}Vis the set of(1,20​δ)(1,20\delta)–quasigeodesic raysγ\gammarepresenting the boundary pointλV\lambda^{V}and avoiding the neighbourhood ofρVU\rho^{U}_{V}of radiusℰ0:=ℰ+Morseδ​(1,20​δ)\mathcal{E}_{0}:=\mathcal{E}+\textup{{Morse}}_{\delta}(1,20\delta). Recall that in this case,πU,x0​(λ)\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda)is defined to beρUV​(λVU)\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda^{U}_{V}), whose diameter we need to bound. Letγ\gammabe a ray in𝒞​V\mathcal{C}Vas above. Leta,b∈γa,b\in\gamma. Letβ\betabe a geodesic in𝒞​V\mathcal{C}Vfromaatobb. Sinceβ\betalies in theMorseδ​(1,20​δ)\textup{{Morse}}_{\delta}(1,20\delta)–neighbourhood ofγ\gamma, it cannot comeℰ\mathcal{E}–close toρVU\rho^{U}_{V}, so by the bounded geodesic image axiom ([BHS19, Defn. 1.1.(7)]),dU​(ρUV​(a),ρUV​(b))≤ℰd_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(a),\rho^{V}_{U}(b))\leq\mathcal{E}. Since this works for arbitrarya,ba,b, we havediam⁡(πU​(γ))≤ℰ\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\pi_{U}(\gamma))\leq\mathcal{E}. Now letγ′\gamma^{\prime}be some other(1,20​E)(1,20E)–quasigeodesic ray in𝒞​V\mathcal{C}Vavoiding𝒩Morseδ​(1,20​δ)+ℰ​(ρVU)\mathcal{N}_{\textup{{Morse}}_{\delta}(1,20\delta)+\mathcal{E}}(\rho^{U}_{V})and representingλV\lambda^{V}. SinceρVU\rho^{U}_{V}is bounded andγ,γ′\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}are at finite Hausdorff distance, there exista∈γ,a′∈γ′a\in\gamma,a^{\prime}\in\gamma^{\prime}such that every geodesic in𝒞​V\mathcal{C}Vfromaatoa′a^{\prime}avoids𝒩ℰ​(ρVU)\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{E}}(\rho^{U}_{V}). Henced​(ρUV​(γ),ρUV​(γ′))≤ℰd(\rho^{V}_{U}(\gamma),\rho^{V}_{U}(\gamma^{\prime}))\leq\mathcal{E}, by another application of bounded geodesic image. Thusdiam⁡(ρUV​(λVU))≤3​ℰ\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda^{U}_{V}))\leq 3\mathcal{E}.

Combining the three cases givesdiam⁡(πU,x0​(λ))≤2​(α+ξ)+3​ℰ\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda))\leq 2(\alpha+\xi)+3\mathcal{E}.
∎

## Definition\thethm(HHS constant).

FixE≥max⁡{q,K,δ,ξ,ξ′,κ0,α,ℰ}E\geq\max\{q,K,\delta,\xi,\xi^{\prime},\kappa_{0},\alpha,\mathcal{E}\};EEis theHHS constant.
∎

## Remark\thethm.

Definition2.2is almost identical to what is usually referred to as the HHS constant (see e.g[BHS19, Remark 1.6]) except for the condition thatE≥ξ′E\geq\xi^{\prime}which accounts for the boundary projections. As usual, since all of the statements in[BHS19, Defn. 1.1]involving the quantities used to defineEEremain true when the quantity in question is replaced byEE, we henceforth work withEEinstead ofκ0,α\kappa_{0},\alpha, etc.
∎

## Definition\thethm(Complexity).

We denote the complexity of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})byχ\chi. Note that it depends only on the poset(𝔖,⊑)(\mathfrak{S},\sqsubseteq).
∎

## Definition\thethm(HHS parameters).

TheHHS parametersof(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})are: the HHS constantEE, the complexityχ\chi, and the uniqueness functionθu\theta_{u}.
∎

When we say that some quantityCCdepends only on the HHS parameters, we mean thatCCis a function ofE,χ,E,\chi,andθu\theta_{u}. For brevity, when working in a fixed HHS, we say that a quantity isuniformto mean that it depends only on the HHS parameters.

## Definition\thethm(Adjusting the boundary projections).

In the interest of only using the constantEEfrom now on, we make a small change to the definition of the boundary projections. Ifλ∈∂X\lambda\in\partial XandV⋤UV\sqsubsetneq Ufor someU∈supp⁡(λ)U\in\operatorname{supp}(\lambda)then letλ′UV≔λUV−𝒩10​E​(ρUV){\lambda^{\prime}}^{V}_{U}\coloneqq\lambda^{V}_{U}-\mathcal{N}_{10E}(\rho^{V}_{U}). DefineπU​(λ)′≔ρVU​(λ′UV)\pi_{U}(\lambda)^{\prime}\coloneqq\rho^{U}_{V}({\lambda^{\prime}}^{V}_{U}). ThenπU​(λ)′⊆πU​(λ)\pi_{U}(\lambda)^{\prime}\subseteq\pi_{U}(\lambda)so its diameter is still bounded byEE. We replaceλUV\lambda^{V}_{U}byλ′UV{\lambda^{\prime}}^{V}_{U}andπU​(λ)\pi_{U}(\lambda)byπU​(λ)′\pi_{U}(\lambda)^{\prime}.
∎

## 2.3.HHS automorphisms and HHGs

We work with the following notion ofHHS automorphismsintroduced in[PS23, Sec. 2], which is simpler to work with than the original formulation (and no less general, by[DHS20, Sec. 2.1]).

## Definition\thethm(HHS automorphism).

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS. A groupHHacts on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})byHHS automorphismsif there is an actionH→S​y​m​(X)H\to Sym(X), an actionH→S​y​m​(𝔖)H\to Sym(\mathfrak{S})preserving the relations⊑,⊥,⋔\sqsubseteq,\bot,\pitchfork, and, for eachU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}and eachh∈Hh\in H, an isometryh:𝒞​U→𝒞​h​Uh:\mathcal{C}U\to\mathcal{C}hUsuch that all of the following hold:
- (1)

IfU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}andg,h∈Hg,h\in H, then the isometryg​h:𝒞​U→𝒞​g​h​Ugh:\mathcal{C}U\to\mathcal{C}ghUis the composition ofg:𝒞​h​U→𝒞​g​h​Ug:\mathcal{C}hU\to\mathcal{C}ghUwithh:𝒞​U→𝒞​h​Uh:\mathcal{C}U\to\mathcal{C}hU.
- (2)

πg​U​(g​x)=g​(πU​(x))\pi_{gU}(gx)=g(\pi_{U}(x))for allx∈X,U∈𝔖,g∈Hx\in X,U\in\mathfrak{S},g\in H.
- (3)

ρg​Vg​U=g​(ρVU)\rho^{gU}_{gV}=g(\rho^{U}_{V})wheneverU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq VorU⋔VU\pitchfork V.
∎

IfHHacts by HHS automorphisms, the distance formula (TheoremA.1) implies that eachh∈Hh\in Hacts onXXas a quasi-isometry with constants depending on the HHS parameters only, but in this paper, it will always suffice to work with groupsHHof HHS automorphisms with the additional property that theHH–action on(X,d)(X,d)is isometric. A special case is:

## Definition\thethm(HHG).

LetGGbe a group and letddbe a finitely generated word metric onGG. Suppose that(G,d)(G,d)admits an HHS structure(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S})on whichGGacts by HHS automorphisms in such a way that the associatedGG–action on the space(G,d)(G,d)is the left-multiplication action, and theGG–action on𝔖\mathfrak{S}is cofinite. Then(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S})is ahierarchically hyperbolic group (HHG) structureforGG, andGGis ahierarchically hyperbolic group (HHG)if it has an HHG structure. We say that the pair(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S})isan HHGin this situation.
∎

Any HHS automorphismggextends to the HHS boundary: ifλ∈∂X\lambda\in\partial Xis as in Definition2.1theng​λ∈∂Xg\lambda\in\partial Xis determined by the supportg​supp⁡(λ)g\operatorname{supp}(\lambda), the boundary pointsg​λU∈∂𝒞​g​Ug\lambda^{U}\in{\partial}\mathcal{C}gUfor allg​U∈g​supp⁡(λ)gU\in g\operatorname{supp}(\lambda)and the constantsag​U≔aUa_{gU}\coloneqq a_{U}for allg​U∈g​supp⁡(λ)gU\in g\operatorname{supp}(\lambda).

## Lemma\thethm(Equivariant downwardρ\rhomaps).

Up to uniformly modifying the HHS parameters, we can assume that
- •

πU​(x)⊆ρUV​(πV​(x))\pi_{U}(x)\subseteq\rho^{V}_{U}(\pi_{V}(x))wheneverx∈Xx\in XandU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq V.
- •

ifggis an HHS automorphism ofXXandU,V∈𝔖U,V\in\mathfrak{S}satisfyU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq V, thenρg​Ug​V​(g​z)=g​ρUV​(z)\rho^{gV}_{gU}(gz)=g\rho^{V}_{U}(z)

for allz∈𝒞​V−𝒩10​E​(ρUV)z\in\mathcal{C}V-\mathcal{N}_{10E}(\rho^{V}_{U}).

## Proof.

Letx∈Xx\in Xand letU,V∈𝔖U,V\in\mathfrak{S}satisfyU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq V. Letz∈𝒞​Vz\in\mathcal{C}V, and suppose thatdV​(z,ρVU)≥10​Ed_{V}(z,\rho^{U}_{V})\geq 10E. Letxz≔{y∈X:dV​(y,z)≤E}x_{z}\coloneqq\{y\in X:d_{V}(y,z)\leq E\}, which is non-empty by the coarse surjectivity ofπV\pi_{V}. LetϱUV​(z)=⋃x∈xzπU​(x)\varrho^{V}_{U}(z)=\bigcup_{x\in x_{z}}\pi_{U}(x). The consistency and bounded geodesic image axioms ([BHS19, Defn. 1.1.(4),(6)]) imply thatϱUV​(z)\varrho^{V}_{U}(z)has diameter bounded in terms ofEE, and lies uniformly close (in terms ofEE) toρUV​(z)\rho^{V}_{U}(z). In particular, we can (and do) replaceρUV\rho^{V}_{U}byϱUV\varrho^{V}_{U}in the HHS structure(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})and still have an HHS whose parameters are bounded in terms of the original ones. (Forz∈𝒞​Vz\in\mathcal{C}VwithdV​(z,ρVU)<10​Ed_{V}(z,\rho^{U}_{V})<10E, letϱUV​(z)=ρUV​(z)\varrho^{V}_{U}(z)=\rho^{V}_{U}(z).)

Letggbe an HHS automorphism and letz∈𝒞​Vz\in\mathcal{C}VsatisfydV​(z,ρVU)≥10​Ed_{V}(z,\rho^{U}_{V})\geq 10E. Definition2.3givesdg​V​(g​z,ρg​Vg​U)≥10​Ed_{gV}(gz,\rho^{gU}_{gV})\geq 10Eandg​xz=xg​zgx_{z}=x_{gz}, soρg​Ug​V​(g​z)=πg​U​(g​xz)=g​πU​(xz)=g​ρUV​(z)\rho^{gV}_{gU}(gz)=\pi_{gU}(gx_{z})=g\pi_{U}(x_{z})=g\rho^{V}_{U}(z).
∎

We will henceforth assume that the downwardsρ\rhomaps are defined as in the proof of Lemma2.3and thus satisfy its conclusions. As a result, the boundary projections are also equivariant with respect to HHS automorphisms:

## Lemma\thethm.

Letλ∈∂X\lambda\in\partial Xbe a point in the HHS boundary ofXX, letggbe an HHS automorphism and letU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}. Theng​πU,x0​(λ)=πg​U,g​x0​(g​λ)g\pi_{U,x_{0}}(\lambda)=\pi_{gU,gx_{0}}(g\lambda)for allx0∈Xx_{0}\in X.

## Proof.

The only case which is not covered by Definition2.3is ifU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq Vfor some (necessarily unique)V∈supp⁡(U)V\in\operatorname{supp}(U). In this caseπU​(λ)=ρUV​(λVU)\pi_{U}(\lambda)=\rho^{V}_{U}(\lambda^{U}_{V}),πg​U=ρg​Ug​V​(λg​Vg​U)\pi_{gU}=\rho^{gV}_{gU}(\lambda^{gU}_{gV})andλVU⊆𝒞​V−𝒩10​E​(ρVU)\lambda^{U}_{V}\subseteq\mathcal{C}V-\mathcal{N}_{10E}(\rho^{U}_{V}),λg​Vg​U⊆𝒞​g​V−𝒩10​E​(ρg​Vg​U)\lambda^{gU}_{gV}\subseteq\mathcal{C}gV-\mathcal{N}_{10E}(\rho^{gU}_{gV}). By Lemma2.3it follows thatg​πU​(λ)=πg​U​(g​λ)g\pi_{U}(\lambda)=\pi_{gU}(g\lambda).
∎

## 2.4.Realisation

We recall from[BHS19]the definition of aconsistent tuple, and therealisation theoremthat, roughly speaking, characterises the image of∏UπU:X→∏U𝒞​U\prod_{U}\pi_{U}:X\to\prod_{U}\mathcal{C}Uas the “solution set” of the consistency inequalities. The following is[BHS19, Defn. 1.17]:

## Definition\thethm(Consistent tuple).

Letκ≥0\kappa\geq 0. Letb→=(bU)U∈𝔖∈∏U∈𝔖2𝒞​U\vec{b}=(b_{U})_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\in\prod_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}2^{\mathcal{C}U}. Thenb→\vec{b}isκ\kappa–admissibleifdiam⁡(bU)≤κ\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(b_{U})\leq\kappaanddU​(bU,πU​(X))≤κd_{U}(b_{U},\pi_{U}(X))\leq\kappafor allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}. Ifb→\vec{b}isκ\kappa–admissible, then it isκ\kappa–consistentif
- •

min⁡{dU​(ρUV,bU),dV​(ρVU,bV)}≤κ\min\{d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U},b_{U}),d_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V},b_{V})\}\leq\kappawheneverU⋔VU\pitchfork V, and
- •

min⁡{dV​(ρVU,bV),diam⁡(ρUV​(bV)∪bU)}≤κ\min\{d_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V},b_{V}),\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\rho^{V}_{U}(b_{V})\cup b_{U})\}\leq\kappawheneverU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq V.∎

Next is the realisation theorem, which is Theorem 3.1 in[BHS19]. Following[CRHK24, Thm. 12.5], we have restated it to make explicit what the quantities involved depend on; as noted in[CRHK24], the constants in question are easy to extract from the proof in[BHS19].{thm}

[Realisation]
Let(𝒳,𝔖)({\mathcal{X}},\mathfrak{S})be a hierarchically hyperbolic space. Then there existsr0r_{0}, depending only on the HHS parameters, such that the following holds. Letκ≥1\kappa\geq 1and let(bV)V∈𝔖∈∏V∈𝔖𝒞​V(b_{V})_{V\in\mathfrak{S}}\in\prod_{V\in\mathfrak{S}}{\mathcal{C}}Vbe aκ\kappa–consistent,κ\kappa–admissible tuple. Then there existsx∈𝒳x\in{\mathcal{X}}such thatdV​(x,bV)≤r0​κd_{V}(x,b_{V})\leq r_{0}\kappafor allV∈𝔖V\in\mathfrak{S}.

## 2.5.Coarse median

We use the notion of acoarse median spacefrom[Bow13, Sec. 8], although we almost always work with special cases — median spaces or HHSes — rather than the general definition. See[NWZ21]for more background oncoarse median algebras/spaces.

## Definition\thethm(Coarse median space).

A metric space(Ω,d)(\Omega,d)iscoarse medianwithparameterκ:ℤ≥0→[0,∞)\kappa:\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\to[0,\infty)if there is a mapμ:Ω3→Ω\mu:\Omega^{3}\to\Omega(thecoarse median) such that:
- (C1)

We haved​(μ​(x,y,z),μ​(x′,y′,z′))≤κ​(0)​(d​(x,x′)+d​(y,y′)+d​(z,z′))+κ​(0)d(\mu(x,y,z),\mu(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}))\leq\kappa(0)\left(d(x,x^{\prime})+d(y,y^{\prime})+d(z,z^{\prime})\right)+\kappa(0)for allx,y,z,x′,y′,z′∈Ωx,y,z,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}\in\Omega.
- (C2)

For alln∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}and allA⊆ΩA\subseteq\Omegawith|A|≤n|A|\leq n, there is a finite median algebra(M,η)(M,\eta)and mapsf:M→Ωf:M\to\Omegaandf¯:A→M\bar{f}:A\to Msuch thatd​(μ​(f​(x),f​(y),f​(z)),f​(η​(x,y,z)))≤κ​(n)d(\mu(f(x),f(y),f(z)),f(\eta(x,y,z)))\leq\kappa(n)for allx,y,z∈Mx,y,z\in M, andd​(a,f​f¯​(a))≤κ​(n)d(a,f\bar{f}(a))\leq\kappa(n)for alla∈Aa\in A.
∎

Next is the coarse median on an HHSXX, from[BHS19, Sec. 7](or[CRHK24, Sec. 14]).

## Construction\thethm(HHS coarse median).

Letx,y,z∈Xx,y,z\in X. For eachU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}, letmUm_{U}be the set ofp∈𝒞​Up\in\mathcal{C}Usuch thatdU​(p,γ)≤10​Ed_{U}(p,\gamma)\leq 10Efor any geodesicγ\gammaof𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uwith endpoints inπU​(x)∪πU​(y)∪πU​(z)\pi_{U}(x)\cup\pi_{U}(y)\cup\pi_{U}(z). Let(bU)U∈𝔖(b_{U})_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}be a tuple withbU∈mUb_{U}\in m_{U}for allUU. By[BHS19, Lem. 2.6], there existsκ\kappasuch that any such(bU)U∈𝔖(b_{U})_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}isκ\kappa–consistent, and the proof of the same lemma shows we can takeκ=100​E\kappa=100E. Hence, the realisation theorem (Theorem2.4) provides a pointm∈Xm\in Xsuch thatdU​(m,bU)≤100​E​r0d_{U}(m,b_{U})\leq 100Er_{0}, wherer0r_{0}is the constant, depending only on the HHS parameters, from Theorem2.4.

Letμ​(x,y,z)\mu(x,y,z)be the set of allm∈Xm\in Xsuch thatd​(m,mU)≤100​E​r0d(m,m_{U})\leq 100Er_{0}for allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}. The above discussion shows thatμ​(x,y,z)≠∅\mu(x,y,z)\neq\emptyset. Now, ifm,m′∈μ​(x,y,z)m,m^{\prime}\in\mu(x,y,z), then for allUU, we havedU​(m,m′)≤200​E​(r0+1)d_{U}(m,m^{\prime})\leq 200E(r_{0}+1), sod​(m,m′)≤θu​(200​E​(r0+1))d(m,m^{\prime})\leq\theta_{u}(200E(r_{0}+1)), whereθu\theta_{u}is the uniqueness function (an HHS parameter). Thusdiam⁡(μ​(x,y,z))\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\mu(x,y,z))is bounded, independently ofx,y,zx,y,z, in terms of the HHS parameters only. Henceμ\muis a well-defined map sending points inX3X^{3}to uniformly bounded sets inXX.

In summary, Construction2.5provides constantsC0,C1C_{0},C_{1}, depending only on the HHS parameters, and a mapμ:X3→2X\mu:X^{3}\to 2^{X}satisfying both of the following conditions:
- •

diam⁡(μ​(x,y,z))≤C0\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\mu(x,y,z))\leq C_{0}for allx,y,z∈Xx,y,z\in X.
- •

For allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}andx,y,z∈Xx,y,z\in X, the following holds. Leta,b∈{x,y,z}a,b\in\{x,y,z\}be distinct. ThendU​(μ​(x,y,z),γ)≤C1d_{U}(\mu(x,y,z),\gamma)\leq C_{1}, whereC1C_{1}is any𝒞​U\mathcal{C}U–geodesic joining a point inπU​(a)\pi_{U}(a)to a point inπU​(b)\pi_{U}(b).

If a groupGGacts on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})by HHS automorphisms, then the coarse mapμ\muisGG–equivariant (for the diagonal action onX3X^{3}and the action on2X2^{X}induced by theGG–action onXX).

In[CRHK24, Sec. 14], it is observed that if(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S})is an HHG then we can, moreover, assume thatμ\muis a map fromX3→XX^{3}\to X(rather than a coarse map) by equivariantly choosing points in the bounded setsμ​(x,y,z)\mu(x,y,z)defined above. This can be done, more generally, as long asGGacts on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})by HHS automorphisms in such a way that theGG–action onXXis free. Later, we will typically reduce to the case of free actions, and in such situations we will assume thatμ\muis an equivariantmap, not just an equivariant coarse map.

## Remark\thethm.

Thatμ\musatisfies Definition2.5follows from the cubical approximation theorem explained in SectionA.2; see CorollaryA.2.
∎

## Definition\thethm(Quasimedian map).

LetΛ,Ω\Lambda,\Omegabe coarse median spaces with coarse mediansλ,ω\lambda,\omegarespectively; letddbe the metric onΩ\Omega. Letκ≥0\kappa\geq 0. A mapf:Λ→Ωf:\Lambda\to\Omegaisκ\kappa–quasimedianifd​(ω​(f​(a),f​(b),f​(c)),f​(λ​(a,b,c)))≤κd(\omega(f(a),f(b),f(c)),f(\lambda(a,b,c)))\leq\kappafor alla,b,c∈Λa,b,c\in\Lambda.
∎

The next two definitions help to interpret HHS notions in terms of the coarse median.

## Definition\thethm(Median quasiconvexity).

LetΩ\Omegabe a coarse median space with coarse medianω\omegaand metricdd. Givenκ≥0\kappa\geq 0, a subsetY⊆ΩY\subseteq\Omegaisκ\kappa–median-quasiconvexifd​(ω​(y,y′,x),Y)≤κd(\omega(y,y^{\prime},x),Y)\leq\kappafor ally,y′∈Yy,y^{\prime}\in Yandx∈Ωx\in\Omega.
∎

## Definition\thethm(Coarse subalgebra).

LetΩ\Omegabe a coarse median space with coarse medianω\omegaand metricdd. Givenκ≥0\kappa\geq 0, aκ\kappa–coarse median subalgebraofΩ\Omegais a subsetY⊆ΩY\subseteq\Omegasuch thatd​(ω​(y,y′,y′′),Y)≤κd(\omega(y,y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime}),Y)\leq\kappafor ally,y′,y′′∈Yy,y^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in Y.
∎

## 2.6.Hierarchical quasiconvexity and related notions

We recall the notion of hierarchical quasiconvexity from[BHS19, Sec. 5]:

## Definition\thethm.

Given a functionκ:[0,∞)→[0,∞)\kappa:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty), a subsetY⊆XY\subseteq Xisκ\kappa–hierarchically quasiconvex (HQC)if both of the following hold:
- •

πU​(Y)\pi_{U}(Y)isκ​(0)\kappa(0)–quasiconvex in theEE–hyperbolic space𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Ufor allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S};
- •

for allr≥0r\geq 0, ifx∈Xx\in XsatisfiesdU​(x,Y)≤rd_{U}(x,Y)\leq rfor allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}, thend​(x,Y)≤κ​(r)d(x,Y)\leq\kappa(r).∎

We next have[RST23, Prop. 5.11], relating hierarchical and coarse median quasiconvexity:

## Proposition\thethm.

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS and letY⊆XY\subseteq X. Then both of the following hold:
- (1)

For allκ:[0,∞)→[0,∞)\kappa:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty), there existsC≥0C\geq 0, depending only onκ\kappaand the HHS parameters, such that, ifYYisκ\kappa–hierarchically quasiconvex, thenYYisCC–median quasiconvex.
- (2)

For allC≥0C\geq 0, there existsκ:[0,∞)→[0,∞)\kappa:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty), depending only onCCand the HHS parameters, such that, ifYYisCC–median quasiconvex, then it isκ\kappa–hierarchically quasiconvex.

Proposition2.6is proved usinghierarchy paths, as defined in[BHS19], which generalise the mapping class group notion from[MM00]. We will also use hierarchy paths, so we recall the definition ([BHS19, Defn. 4.2]):

## Definition\thethm(Hierarchy path).

GivenD≥0D\geq 0, aDD–hierarchy pathinXXis a(D,D)(D,D)–quasi-isometric embeddingγ:[0,L]→X\gamma:[0,L]\to X, for someL≥0L\geq 0, with the property thatπW∘γ\pi_{W}\circ\gammais an unparametrised(D,D)(D,D)–quasigeodesic in𝒞​W\mathcal{C}Wfor allW∈𝔖W\in\mathfrak{S}.
∎

Combining[BHS21, Lem. 1.37]with[BHS19, Thm. 4.4]yields:

## Proposition\thethm.

There existsDD, depending only on the HHS parameters, such that any two points inXXare joined by aDD–hierarchy path.

Moreover, for anyD′≥0D^{\prime}\geq 0, there existsC≥0C\geq 0, depending only onD′D^{\prime}and the HHS parameters, such that a(D′,D′)(D^{\prime},D^{\prime})–quasigeodesicγ:[0,L]→X\gamma:[0,L]\to Xis aD′D^{\prime}-hierarchy path providedγ\gammaisCC–quasimedian. Conversely, for allCC, there existsD′′D^{\prime\prime}depending only onCCand the HHS parameters such that anyCC–quasimedian(C,C)(C,C)–quasiisometric embeddingγ:[0,L]→X\gamma:[0,L]\to Xis aD′′D^{\prime\prime}–hierarchy path.

Hierarchical quasiconvexity (equivalently, coarse median quasiconvexity) generalises quasiconvexity in hyperbolic spaces and “coarsifies” the notion of median convexity in median metric spaces. Accordingly, the coarse closest point projection to a quasiconvex subset of a hyperbolic space generalises to HQC subsets of HHSes in a way that mimics gate maps in median spaces, using the following construction from[BHS19, Sec. 5].

## Construction\thethm(HQC gate maps).

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS and letY⊆XY\subseteq Xbe aκ\kappa–HQC subset. Define𝔤Y:X→2Y\mathfrak{g}_{Y}:X\to 2^{Y}as follows. For eachU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}, letpUY:𝒞​U→2πU​(Y)p^{Y}_{U}:\mathcal{C}U\to 2^{\pi_{U}(Y)}be the coarse closest-point projection, which takes points to sets of diameter bounded in terms ofEEandκ​(0)\kappa(0). Fixx∈Xx\in X. Lemma 5.3 of[BHS19]provides a constantκ′\kappa^{\prime}, depending on the HHS parameters and the functionκ\kappabut not onxx, such that any tuple(qU)U∈𝔖(q_{U})_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}withqU∈pUY​(πU​(x))q_{U}\in p^{Y}_{U}(\pi_{U}(x))for allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}isκ′\kappa^{\prime}–consistent. Hence there existsκ′′\kappa^{\prime\prime}, depending on the HHS parameters andκ\kappaonly, such that there exists at least onex¯∈Y\bar{x}\in Ysuch thatdU​(x¯,pUY​(πU​(x)))≤κ′′d_{U}(\bar{x},p^{Y}_{U}(\pi_{U}(x)))\leq\kappa^{\prime\prime}for allUU. Let𝔤Y​(x)\mathfrak{g}_{Y}(x)be the set of all suchx¯\bar{x}. As noted in[BHS19, Defn. 5.4, Lem. 5.5], up to uniformly enlargingκ′′\kappa^{\prime\prime}, we havediam⁡(𝔤Y​(x))≤κ′′\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\mathfrak{g}_{Y}(x))\leq\kappa^{\prime\prime}for allx∈Xx\in X, and the coarse map𝔤Y\mathfrak{g}_{Y}is(κ′′,κ′′)(\kappa^{\prime\prime},\kappa^{\prime\prime})–coarsely lipschitz,κ′′\kappa^{\prime\prime}-quasimedian, anddY​(𝔤Y​(y),y)≤κ′′d_{Y}(\mathfrak{g}_{Y}(y),y)\leq\kappa^{\prime\prime}for ally∈Yy\in Y.

One can uniformly perturb a gate map𝔤Y\mathfrak{g}_{Y}so that it is a coarsely lipschitz retraction ontoYY. Thus HQC subsets arecoarse lipschitz retracts.

## Lemma\thethm(Equivariant gate maps).

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS. LetGGbe a group equipped with an action on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})by HHS automorphisms. Suppose thatY⊆XY\subseteq Xisκ\kappa–HQC andGG–invariant. Then the gate map𝔤Y:X→2Y\mathfrak{g}_{Y}:X\to 2^{Y}from Construction2.6isGG–equivariant. Moreover, if theGG–action onXXis free, then there is aGG–equivariant map𝔤Y′:X→Y\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{Y}:X\to Ysuch thatsupx∈Xdiam⁡(𝔤Y​(x)∪𝔤Y′​(x))\sup_{x\in X}\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\mathfrak{g}_{Y}(x)\cup\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{Y}(x))is bounded above in terms ofκ\kappaand the HHS parameters.

By Lemma2.6, in any situation where theGG–action onXXis free and preservesYY, we will assume that𝔤Y\mathfrak{g}_{Y}is aGG–equivariant mapX→YX\to Y(not just a coarse map) with the properties from Construction2.6, withκ′′\kappa^{\prime\prime}depending on the HHS parameters andκ\kappaonly.

## Proof of Lemma2.6.

From Definition2.3and the assumption thatG​Y=YGY=Y, for allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}andg∈Gg\in G, we haveπg​U​(Y)=g​(πU​(Y))\pi_{gU}(Y)=g(\pi_{U}(Y)). From the definition of coarse closest-point projection in a hyperbolic space, it follows thatpg​UY(πg​U(gx))=pg​UY(g(πU(x))=g(pUY(x))p^{Y}_{gU}(\pi_{gU}(gx))=p^{Y}_{gU}(g(\pi_{U}(x))=g(p^{Y}_{U}(x))for allx∈Xx\in X, so Construction2.6implies that𝔤Y​(g​x)=g​𝔤Y​(x)\mathfrak{g}_{Y}(gx)=g\mathfrak{g}_{Y}(x)for allg∈G,x∈Xg\in G,x\in X. Now suppose that theGG–action onXXis free. Let{xi}i∈I⊆X\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq Xcontain exactly one point ofXXin eachGG–orbit, and for eachi∈Ii\in I, choose𝔤Y′​(xi)∈𝔤Y​(x)\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{Y}(x_{i})\in\mathfrak{g}_{Y}(x)arbitrarily. Givenx∈Xx\in X, there is a uniqueg∈Gg\in Gandi∈Ii\in Iwithx=g​xix=gx_{i}, so set𝔤Y′​(x)=g​𝔤Y′​(xi)\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{Y}(x)=g\mathfrak{g}_{Y}^{\prime}(x_{i}). This gives an equivariant map𝔤Y′:X→Y\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{Y}:X\to Y, anddiam⁡(𝔤Y​(x)∪𝔤Y′​(x))=diam⁡(𝔤Y​(x))≤κ′′\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\mathfrak{g}_{Y}(x)\cup\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}_{Y}(x))=\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\mathfrak{g}_{Y}(x))\leq\kappa^{\prime\prime}for allx∈Xx\in X, as claimed.
∎

An important feature of hierarchically quasiconvex subsets is that they can be equipped with an induced HHS structure. The following holds by[BHS19, Lem. 5.6, Rem. 5.7]. The fact that the action ofGGonYYis by HHS automorphisms follows from the same considerations as in the equivariance part of the proof of Lemma2.6.

## Lemma\thethm(HHS structure inherited by HQC set).

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS, letκ:[0,∞)→[0,∞)\kappa:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)be a function, and letY⊆XY\subseteq Xbe aκ\kappa-hierarchically quasiconvex subspace. LetGGbe a group acting onXXby HHS automorphisms such thatG​Y=YGY=Y. Then there is an HHS structure𝔖Y\mathfrak{S}_{Y}onYYsuch that the action ofGGonYYis by HHS automorphisms and theπU\pi_{U}–projection maps are coarsely surjective. The HHS parameters of(Y,𝔖Y)(Y,\mathfrak{S}_{Y})depend only on the HHS parameters of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})andκ\kappa.

## Remark\thethm.

The structure(Y,𝔖Y)(Y,\mathfrak{S}_{Y})from Lemma2.6can be constructed in various ways. If we do not require coarse surjectivity of the projections, then it has the following particularly simple description. Let𝔖Y=𝔖\mathfrak{S}_{Y}=\mathfrak{S}(as a set with relations⊑,⊥,⋔\sqsubseteq,\bot,\pitchfork), for eachU∈𝔖YU\in\mathfrak{S}_{Y}, let𝒞Y​U=𝒞​U\mathcal{C}_{Y}U=\mathcal{C}U, and letπUY:Y→𝒞​U\pi_{U}^{Y}:Y\to\mathcal{C}UbeπUY=πU|Y\pi^{Y}_{U}=\pi_{U}|_{Y}. Recalling that the definition of an HHS does not require coarse surjectivity of projections, but only quasiconvexity of their images,(Y,𝔖Y)(Y,\mathfrak{S}_{Y})is an HHS and in particular the distance formula (TheoremA.1) applies. This description is convenient for some distance estimates in the proof of Proposition5, and we do not need it elsewhere.

However, if one wishes to stay within the subclass of HHSes with uniformly coarsely surjective projections, then one can simply normalise, as in[BHS19, Rem. 1.4]. In this procedure, ifV⋤UV\sqsubsetneq UorV⋔UV\pitchfork U, then replaceρUV\rho^{V}_{U}with its image under the coarse closest-point projection𝒞​U→2πU​(Y)\mathcal{C}U\to 2^{\pi_{U}(Y)}(using thatπU​(Y)\pi_{U}(Y)is uniformly quasiconvex in𝒞​U\mathcal{C}U).
∎

## 2.7.Existential hierarchical quasiconvexity

Here is the weaker version of hierarchical quasiconvexity used in our main result:

## Definition\thethm(EHQC).

Letk≥1,r≥0k\geq 1,r\geq 0. A subspaceY⊆XY\subseteq Xis(k,r)(k,r)–existentially hierarchically quasiconvex (EHQC)if, for ally1,y2∈Yy_{1},y_{2}\in Y, there exists akk-hierarchy pathγ\gammafromy1y_{1}toy2y_{2}withγ⊆𝒩r​(Y)\gamma\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{r}(Y).
∎

## Remark\thethm.

Observe that for anyk≥1,r≥0k\geq 1,r\geq 0, there existsk′k^{\prime}such thatYYis(k,r)(k,r)–EHQC if and only if it is(k′,0)(k^{\prime},0)–EHQC, since one can perturb a hierarchy path to lie inYYat the expense of a controlled change in the hierarchy path constant.
∎

The following is straightforward to check using Proposition2.6and Proposition2.6; it motivates Definition2.7, but we will not use it. Compare[HP22, Lem. 2.11, Prop. 2.8].{cor}

For allCC, there existk,rk,r, depending onCCand the HHS parameters, such that anyCC–coarsely connectedCC–coarse median subalgebra ofXXis(k,r)(k,r)–EHQC.

The converse to the preceding corollary is not true (consider codimension–11affine subspaces ofℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}, and giveℝ3\mathbb{R}^{3}theℓ1\ell_{1}metric), so we have to work with the more general notion of EHQC, and the language of paths, instead of coarse medians and coarse subalgebras.

By the distance formula (see TheoremA.1below),ppis coarsely unique (constants depending only on the HHS parameters) but not necessarily unique. LetFU×{q}F_{U}\times\{q\}be the set of all suchpp, as the tuple(pV)V⊑U(p_{V})_{V\sqsubseteq U}varies. Whenqqis not important, we will denoteFU×{q}F_{U}\times\{q\}byFUF_{U}and refer to it as acoarse parallel copy ofFUF_{U}inPUP_{U}.

It is shown in, for instance,[CRHK24, Sec. 17], that the subspacesPUP_{U}andFU×{q}F_{U}\times\{q\}and{p}×EU\{p\}\times E_{U}are hierarchically quasiconvex, with hierarchical quasiconvexity functions depending only on the HHS parameters. In particular, there is no loss of generality in assuming that each of these subspaces isEE–quasimedian quasiconvex, and we will do this to save notation.

## 2.10.Canonical HHS cone-off

The procedure for coning off certain hierarchically quasiconvex subsets of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})to obtain new HHS structures was introduced in[BHS19, Section 2]and reproved in a slightly stronger form in[CRHK24, Section 19]. Here we adapt the cone-off construction to suit our purposes and use the statement from[CRHK24]to show that the adapted construction has the properties we need.

## Definition\thethm(Cone-off data).

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS, letddbe the metric onXX, letA→Isom⁡(X)A\to\operatorname{Isom}(X)be a group action by HHS automorphisms on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}), and let𝔘⊆𝔖\mathfrak{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}be anAA–invariant subset that is downward-closed in the partial order⊑\sqsubseteq. The tuple((X,𝔖),A,𝔘)((X,\mathfrak{S}),A,\mathfrak{U})is calledcone-off data.
∎

In both cone-off constructions, one creates a new HHS, with anAA–action by HHS automorphisms, where the hierarchically quasiconvex subsetsFU⊂XF_{U}\subset XforU∈𝔘U\in\mathfrak{U}have become bounded, and the new HHS index set is𝔖−𝔘\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U}. The new HHS, as a set, coincides withXX, and the cone-off construction amounts to replacingddwith a new metric. The difference between the construction from[BHS17b,CRHK24]and our adapted version is a technical difference in how the new metric is defined. With the original definition, as noted in[CRHK24], the groupAAdoes act onXX, but the action is by uniform quasi-isometries, not isometries. The purpose of the adapted construction is to keep theAA–action isometric.

We first recall the cone-off metric from[BHS17b,CRHK24]:

## Definition\thethm(Non-canonical𝔘\mathfrak{U}–cone-off).

Givenx,y∈Xx,y\in X, letD​(x,y)≔d​(x,y)D(x,y)\coloneqq d(x,y)unless there existsU∈𝔘U\in\mathfrak{U}ande∈EUe\in E_{U}such that{x,y}⊂FU×{e}\{x,y\}\subset F_{U}\times\{e\}, in which caseD​(x,y)≔min⁡{1,d​(x,y)}D(x,y)\coloneqq\min\{1,d(x,y)\}. Thenon-canonical𝔘\mathfrak{U}–cone-offis the metric spaceX^=(X,d^)\widehat{X}=(X,\widehat{d}), whered^\widehat{d}is the length metric onXXinduced byDD.
∎

## Remark\thethm(Non-canonical).

The phrasenon-canonicalrefers to the fact that the subspacesFUF_{U}ofXXare generally only coarsely well-defined. Because of the arbitrary choices involved in constructing the subspacesFU×{q}F_{U}\times\{q\}, the condition on pairsx,yx,yensuring thatD​(x,y)≤1D(x,y)\leq 1is not alwaysAA–invariant, so theAA–action onX^\widehat{X}may not be isometric.
∎

## Definition\thethm(Canonical𝔘\mathfrak{U}–cone-off).

Givenx,y∈Xx,y\in X, letDˇ​(x,y)≔d​(x,y)\widecheck{D}(x,y)\coloneqq d(x,y)unless both of the following hold:
- •

There existsU∈𝔘U\in\mathfrak{U}such thatx,y∈PUx,y\in P_{U}, and
- •

dV​(x,y)≤10​Ed_{V}(x,y)\leq 10Efor allV∈𝔖V\in\mathfrak{S}such thatV⊥UV\bot U.

Ifx,yx,ysatisfy both conditions, letDˇ​(x,y)≔min⁡{1,d​(x,y)}\widecheck{D}(x,y)\coloneqq\min\{1,d(x,y)\}. Now letdˇ​(x,y)\widecheck{d}(x,y)be the length metric onXXinduced byDˇ\widecheck{D}, and letXˇ=(X,dˇ)\widecheck{X}=(X,\widecheck{d}), which is calledcanonical𝔘\mathfrak{U}–cone-off.
∎

## Lemma\thethm.

TheAA–action onXXinduces an action ofAAonXˇ\widecheck{X}by isometries.

## Proof.

This follows from the fact that theAA–action on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})is by HHS automorphisms and by isometries on(X,d)(X,d). In particular,g​PU=Pg​UgP_{U}=P_{gU}, anddg​V​(g​x,g​y)=dV​(x,y)d_{gV}(gx,gy)=d_{V}(x,y), soDˇ\widecheck{D}isAA–invariant, and hencedˇ\widecheck{d}is also.
∎

## Lemma\thethm.

There existsCC, depending only on the HHS parameters of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}), such that the set-theoretic identity mapXˇ→X^\widecheck{X}\to\widehat{X}is a(C,C)(C,C)–quasi-isometry.

## Proof.

Fixx,y∈Xx,y\in Xand suppose thatD​(x,y)≤1D(x,y)\leq 1. Ifd​(x,y)≤1d(x,y)\leq 1, thenDˇ​(x,y)≤1\widecheck{D}(x,y)\leq 1. The other possibility is that there existsU∈𝔘U\in\mathfrak{U}andq∈PUq\in P_{U}such thatx,y∈FU×{q}x,y\in F_{U}\times\{q\}. By definition, this means thatx,y∈PUx,y\in P_{U}, and for allVVsuch thatV⊥UV\bot U, we havedV​(x,qV)≤Ed_{V}(x,q_{V})\leq EanddV​(y,qV)≤Ed_{V}(y,q_{V})\leq E, sodV​(x,y)≤2​Ed_{V}(x,y)\leq 2E, and henceDˇ​(x,y)≤1\widecheck{D}(x,y)\leq 1.

Conversely, suppose thatDˇ​(x,y)≤1\widecheck{D}(x,y)\leq 1. Ifd​(x,y)≤1d(x,y)\leq 1, thenD​(x,y)≤1D(x,y)\leq 1. Otherwise, there existsU∈𝔘U\in\mathfrak{U}such thatx,y∈PUx,y\in P_{U}anddV​(x,y)≤10​Ed_{V}(x,y)\leq 10EwheneverV⊥UV\bot U. Consider the subspaceFU×{x}F_{U}\times\{x\}. As noted just after Lemma 17.1 in[CRHK24](or see[BHS19, Sec. 5]),FU×{x}F_{U}\times\{x\}is uniformly hierarchically quasiconvex in(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}), and admits a gate map𝔤:X→FU×{x}\mathfrak{g}:X\to F_{U}\times\{x\}that is uniformly coarsely lipschitz and has the following properties, for allz∈Xz\in X:
- •

IfV∈𝔖V\in\mathfrak{S}andV⊑UV\sqsubseteq U, thendV​(z,𝔤​(z))≤r0​Ed_{V}(z,\mathfrak{g}(z))\leq r_{0}E.
- •

IfV∈𝔖V\in\mathfrak{S}andV⊥UV\bot U, thendV​(𝔤​(z),x)≤r0​Ed_{V}(\mathfrak{g}(z),x)\leq r_{0}E.
- •

IfV∈𝔖V\in\mathfrak{S}andU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq VorU⋔VU\pitchfork V, thendV​(𝔤​(z),ρVU)≤r0​Ed_{V}(\mathfrak{g}(z),\rho^{U}_{V})\leq r_{0}E.

In particular,dV​(x,𝔤​(x))≤r0​Ed_{V}(x,\mathfrak{g}(x))\leq r_{0}Efor allV∈𝔖V\in\mathfrak{S}, sod​(x,𝔤​(x))≤θu​(r0​E)d(x,\mathfrak{g}(x))\leq\theta_{u}(r_{0}E). Similarly,dV​(y,𝔤​(y))≤10​E+r0​Ed_{V}(y,\mathfrak{g}(y))\leq 10E+r_{0}Efor allVV, sod​(y,𝔤​(y))≤θu​(10​E+r0​E)d(y,\mathfrak{g}(y))\leq\theta_{u}(10E+r_{0}E). Now,D​(𝔤​(x),𝔤​(y))≤1D(\mathfrak{g}(x),\mathfrak{g}(y))\leq 1by the definition ofDD, soD​(x,y)≤θu​(r0​E)+θu​(10​E+r0​E)+1D(x,y)\leq\theta_{u}(r_{0}E)+\theta_{u}(10E+r_{0}E)+1, which depends only on the HHS parameters.

We have shown that the identity map(X,d^)→(X,dˇ)(X,\widehat{d})\to(X,\widecheck{d})and its inverse are both uniformly coarsely lipschitz, so they are quasi-isometries, as required.
∎{cor}

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}),AA, and𝔘\mathfrak{U}be as in Definition2.10. Then there is an HHS(Xˇ,𝔖−𝔘)(\widecheck{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U}), with HHS parameters depending only on the HHS parameters of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}), such thatAAacts on(Xˇ,𝔖−𝔘)(\widecheck{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U})by HHS automorphisms and onXˇ\widecheck{X}is by isometries, and there is anAA–equivariant quasi-isometryXˇ→X^\widecheck{X}\to\widehat{X}with constants depending only on the HHS parameters.

Finally, the identity mapc:X→Xˇc:X\to\widecheck{X}is coarsely lipschitz and quasimedian, with the constants for both properties depending only on the HHS parameters of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}).

## Proof.

By PropositionA.3, we have a constantCC, depending only on the HHS parameters of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}), such thatAAacts by(C,C)(C,C)–quasi-isometries on the non-canonical cone-offX^\widehat{X}, and this action is by HHS automorphisms on(X^,𝔖−𝔘)(\widehat{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U}).

Lemma2.10implies that the canonical cone-off(Xˇ,𝔖−𝔘)(\widecheck{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U})is an HHS with uniform HHS parameters, defining projectionsπW′:Xˇ→𝒞​W,W∈𝔖−𝔘\pi^{\prime}_{W}:\widecheck{X}\to\mathcal{C}W,\ W\in\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U}by composing the projectionsπW:X^→𝒞​W\pi_{W}:\widehat{X}\to\mathcal{C}Wwith the identityXˇ→X^\widecheck{X}\to\widehat{X}(see also[BHS19, Prop. 1.10]). TheAA–action onXˇ\widecheck{X}is isometric by Lemma2.10. The action is by HHS automorphisms since that holds for(X^,𝔖−𝔘)(\widehat{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U}), and passing toXˇ\widecheck{X}did not change the index set or the hyperbolic spaces, and did not change the projection maps at the set-theoretic level.

Finally, the map identity mapccis coarsely lipschitz with uniform constants as an immediate consequence of Definition2.10and the fact thatXXis a uniform quasigeodesic space. The fact thatccis quasimedian follows from the characterisation of the coarse median in an HHS (Construction2.5) and the fact that, forU∈𝔖−𝔘U\in\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U}, the projections to𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Ucoincide for the HHS structures onXXandXˇ\widecheck{X}. This completes the proof.
∎

## Remark\thethm.

In our applications, the input HHSXXwill be the vertex set of a graph, andddwill be the usual graph metric obtained by assigning edges length11. In this setting,Xˇ\widecheck{X}is obtained by adding unit length edges joining any two verticesx,yx,ywithDˇ​(x,y)≤1\widecheck{D}(x,y)\leq 1, and taking the resulting graph metric on the vertex set.

[Uniformly bounded orbits] 
Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS and assumeXXis a graph. There exists a constantBBdepending only on the HHS parameters such that, ifH≤Isom⁡(X,d)H\leq\operatorname{Isom}(X,d)is a group of HHS automorphisms of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})acting onXXwith bounded orbits, then there existsx0∈Xx_{0}\in{X}such thatdiam⁡(H⋅x0)≤B\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(H\cdot x_{0})\leq B.

By[Lan13, Proposition 1.2], there is a pointf∈Env​(X)f\in\mathrm{Env}(X)which is fixed byGG. Letx∈Xx\in Xbe such thatd∞​(i​(x),f)≤M1d_{\infty}(i(x),f)\leq M_{1}. Thendiamd⁡(G⋅x)≤M2​diamσ⁡(G⋅x)+M2≤2​M2​M1+M2≕B,\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}_{d}(G\cdot x)\leq M_{2}\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}_{\sigma}(G\cdot x)+M_{2}\leq 2M_{2}M_{1}+M_{2}\eqcolon B,

which depends only on the HHS parameters since the same is true ofM1,M2M_{1},M_{2}.
