## 2.12.Simplifying actions

Here are two more or less standard lemmas that will be convenient in places where we want to perturb maps to make them equivariant.

## Lemma\thethm.

For everyq≥1q\geq 1, there existsr≥1r\geq 1, depending only onqq, such that the following holds. Let(X,d)(X,d)be aqq–quasigeodesic space on which the groupGGacts by isometries. Then there is a graphΩ\Omegasuch thatGGacts onΩ\Omegaby graph automorphisms and there is aGG–equivariant mapf:X→Ωf:X\to\Omegathat is anrr–coarsely surjective(r,r)(r,r)–quasi-isometry, whereΩ\Omegais equipped with the usual path-metric in which all edges have unit length. Moreover,Ω\Omegahas vertex setXXand we may takeffto be the inclusionX→ΩX\to\Omega.

## Proof.

This follows from[CdlH16, Lem. 3.B.6].
∎

## Remark\thethm.

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS on whichGGacts isometrically by HHS automorphisms. Then Lemma2.12allows us to remetriseXXas the vertex set of a graph, without changing the projectionsπU:X→𝒞​U\pi_{U}:X\to\mathcal{C}U; by[BHS19, Prop. 1.10],(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})is still an HHS, it is immediate from Definition2.3that the action is still by isometric HHS automorphisms, and the new HHS parameters just depend on the old ones, via the quasi-isometry constantrrfrom Lemma2.12, which just depends on the old HHS parameterqqfrom Subsection2.2. In a few places, it will be convenient to assume thatXXis a graph, and this observation enables it.
∎

## Lemma\thethm(Freeing actions).

LetΩ\Omegabe a simplicial graph and letGGact by graph automorphisms onΩ\Omegawithout inverting edges. Then there is a graphΩ^\widehat{\Omega}, a freeGG–actionG→Aut⁡(Ω^)G\to\operatorname{Aut}(\widehat{\Omega}), and aGG–equivariant surjective mapf:Ω^→Ωf:\widehat{\Omega}\to\Omegathat is a(1,10)(1,10)–quasi-isometry taking vertices to vertices and edges to edges or vertices.

## Proof.

LetVVbe the vertex set ofΩ\Omegaand letEEbe the set of edges. LetΔ​(G)=Cay​(G,G)\Delta(G)=\mathrm{Cay}(G,G)be a complete graph with a free, vertex-transitiveGG–action. TheGG–action onGGby left multiplication extends to an action onΔ​(G)\Delta(G), and we are given aGG–action onΩ\Omega. These two actions yield a diagonal action on the complexΔ​(G)×Ω\Delta(G)\times\Omegaby combinatorial automorphisms.

Let{vi}i∈I⊆V\{v_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subseteq Vcontain exactly one element ofVVin eachGG–orbit. DefineV^⊆G×V\widehat{V}\subseteq G\times Vas follows. Giveng∈Gg\in Gandv∈Vv\in V, leti∈Ii\in Ibe the unique element such thatv=h​viv=hv_{i}for someh∈Gh\in G. Then(g,v)∈V^(g,v)\in\widehat{V}if and only ifh−1​g∈StabG⁡(vi)h^{-1}g\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(v_{i}). Note that if(g,h​vi)∈V^(g,hv_{i})\in\widehat{V}anda∈Ga\in G, then(a​g,a​h​vi)∈V^(ag,ahv_{i})\in\widehat{V}, so theGG–action preservesV^\widehat{V}. The setV^\widehat{V}, with thisGG–action, will be the vertex set ofΩ^\widehat{\Omega}. TheGG–action onV^\widehat{V}is free because the action on the first coordinate is free.

Declare(g,v),(h,w)∈V^(g,v),(h,w)\in\widehat{V}to be adjacent inΩ^\widehat{\Omega}if and only ifv=wv=wor{v,w}∈E\{v,w\}\in E. TheGG–action onV^\widehat{V}preserves these relations and thus extends to a freeGG–action onΩ^\widehat{\Omega}.

The natural projectionΔ​(G)×Ω→Ω\Delta(G)\times\Omega\to\Omegarestricts to aGG–equivariant mapf:Ω^→Ωf:\widehat{\Omega}\to\Omegataking vertices to vertices. Each edge ofΩ^\widehat{\Omega}joining vertices with the same second coordinate gets collapsed to a vertex, and the remaining edges are sent isometrically to edges. Henceffis11–lipschitz. On the other hand, the mapf¯:V→V^\bar{f}:V\to\widehat{V}given byf¯​(h​vi)=(h,h​vi)\bar{f}(hv_{i})=(h,hv_{i})(the map is determined by choices of coset representatives for theStabG⁡(vi)\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(v_{i})) defines a section offfthat is 1–lipschitz and satisfiesdΩ^​(x,f¯​f​(x))≤1d_{\widehat{\Omega}}(x,\bar{f}f(x))\leq 1for allxx. This completes the proof.
∎

## 2.13.Virtually abelian and crystallographic groups

We recall some standard facts about virtually abelian and crystallographic groups. Fixn≥0n\geq 0and letAAbe a virtuallyℤn\mathbb{Z}^{n}group, so that there is a short exact sequenceA0↪A↠FA_{0}\hookrightarrow A\twoheadrightarrow F, whereFFis finite andA0≅ℤnA_{0}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

## Definition\thethm(Point group ofAA).

The conjugation action ofAAonA0A_{0}gives a homomorphismA→G​Ln​(ℤ)A\to GL_{n}(\mathbb{Z})whose image we denote byF¯\bar{F}and call thepoint groupofAA.
∎

Note thatA→G​Ln​(ℤ)A\to GL_{n}(\mathbb{Z})factors through the quotientA→FA\to F, soF¯\bar{F}is finite. Moreover, ifA0′≤A0A_{0}^{\prime}\leq A_{0}is another finite-index free abelian subgroup that is normal inAA, then the conjugation actions ofAAonA0A_{0}andA0′A_{0}^{\prime}make the mapι:A0′↪A0\iota:A_{0}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow A_{0}anAA–equivariant map, whereAAacts onA0′A_{0}^{\prime}via the point groupF¯′=im⁡(A→A​u​t​(A0′))\bar{F}^{\prime}=\operatorname{im}(A\to Aut(A_{0}^{\prime}))and onA0A_{0}viaF¯\bar{F}. Butι\iotaextends to an isomorphismℝn→ℝn\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}, which therefore conjugatesF¯′\bar{F}^{\prime}toF¯\bar{F}. This implies that the point groupF¯≤G​Ln​(ℝ)\bar{F}\leq GL_{n}(\mathbb{R})is, up to conjugacy, independent of the choice ofA0A_{0}.

Virtuallyℤn\mathbb{Z}^{n}groups are related tonn–dimensional crystallographic groups by the following classical fact[Zas48]:

## Lemma\thethm.

IfAAis a virtuallyℤn\mathbb{Z}^{n}group, then there is a finite normal subgroupK≤AK\leq Aand a monomorphismϕ:A/K→Isom⁡(𝔼n)\phi:A/K\to\operatorname{Isom}(\mathbb{E}^{n})whose image isnn–dimensional crystallographic, i.e.ϕ​(A/K)\phi(A/K)acts faithfully on𝔼n\mathbb{E}^{n}and this action is proper and cocompact.

In Section7, we will use these facts via the following lemma:

## Lemma\thethm.

Letn≥0n\geq 0and letKKbe a finite group. Then there are finitely many abstract isomorphism classes of groupsAAsuch thatK⊲AK\triangleleft AandA/KA/Kis isomorphic to annn–dimensional crystallographic group.

## Proof.

Fixnn. LetAAbe as in the statement and letC=A/KC=A/K. By Bieberbach’s theorems,CCbelongs to one of finitely many isomorphism classes. SinceKKis finite andAAis finitely generated, there are finitely many homomorphismsC→Out⁡(K)C\to\operatorname{Out}(K). The centreZ​(K)Z(K)ofKKis finite, so for each way of regardingZ​(K)Z(K)as aCC–module,H2​(C,Z​(K))H^{2}(C,Z(K))is finite, and hence yields at most finitely many possibilities forAA.
∎

In[PS23], Petyt and Spriano characterised crystallographic HHGs, using the following notion, which was also used in[Hag14]and[Hod23], respectively, to characterise crystallographic groups that are cocompactly cubulated and act geometrically on injective spaces.

## Definition\thethm(Hyperoctahedral group).

LetAAbe a virtuallyℤn\mathbb{Z}^{n}group and letF¯\bar{F}be the point group ofAA. ThenAAishyperoctahedralifF¯\bar{F}is conjugate inG​Ln​(ℝ)GL_{n}(\mathbb{R})intoOn​(ℤ)O_{n}(\mathbb{Z}).
∎

We will use this notion in Section7, when we study crystallographicsubgroupsof HHGs.

## 3.An equivariant median model

[store=median approx., note=Equivariant median model]

LetGGbe a group acting onXXby HHS automorphisms and, ifp−,p+∈∂Xp^{-},p^{+}\in\partial X, supposeGGis virtually abelian. Then there is a complete, connected, finite rank, proper median spaceQQ, equipped with an action ofGG, and aGG-equivariantL′L^{\prime}-quasimedian(L′,L′)(L^{\prime},L^{\prime})-quasi-isometryΨ:X→Q\Psi:X\rightarrow Q, where the constantL′≥1L^{\prime}\geq 1depends only on the HHS parameters andMM.

Let(X,𝔖),M,p±,(X,\mathfrak{S}),M,p^{\pm},andGGbe as in Theorem3. Suppose thatp±∈∂Xp^{\pm}\in{\partial}X, andsupp⁡(p−)=supp⁡(p+)\operatorname{supp}(p^{-})=\operatorname{supp}(p^{+}), andπU​(G⋅z0)\pi_{U}(G\cdot z_{0})is unbounded for allU∈supp⁡(p±)U\in\operatorname{supp}(p^{\pm})and some (equivalently any)z0∈Xz_{0}\in X. LetQQbe theGG–median space provided by Theorem3. Then there exist median spacesQ1,…,QrQ_{1},\ldots,Q_{r}such that:
- •

eachQiQ_{i}is quasi-isometric toℝ\mathbb{R}, and
- •

GGacts by isometries on∏i=1rQi\prod_{i=1}^{r}Q_{i}, preserving the product structure, and there is aGG–equivariant isometryQ→∏i=1rQiQ\to\prod_{i=1}^{r}Q_{i},
- •

ifG′≤GG^{\prime}\leq Gis the finite index subgroup which acts trivially on the set of factors, then theG′G^{\prime}-action on eachQiQ_{i}is cocompact.

Moreover, there existsB<∞B<\inftysuch thatdiam⁡(𝒞​V)<B\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\mathcal{C}V)<Bfor allV∈𝔖−supp⁡(p±)V\in\mathfrak{S}-\operatorname{supp}(p^{\pm}).

The quasi-isometry constants and the constantBBin Proposition3are not uniform; they depend on more than the HHS constants andMM.

## Proposition\thethm.

LetGGand(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be as in Theorem3. There exists a constantL≥1L\geq 1depending only on the HHS parameters andMMsuch that the following holds. for allg,h∈Gg,h\in GandU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}the isometryg​h:TU→Tg​h​Ugh:T_{U}\rightarrow T_{ghU}is the compositiong∘hg\circ h.

for allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}the mapfUf_{U}isStabG⁡(U)\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(U)–equivariant.

TUT_{U}is unbounded if and only ifU∈supp⁡(q)U\in\operatorname{supp}(q)for someq∈{p−,p+}∩∂Xq\in\{p^{-},p^{+}\}\cap{\partial}X.

Moreover, lettingp±∈X∪∂Xp^{\pm}\in X\cup{\partial}Xbe as in Theorem3,GGhas a finite index subgroup fixing{p−,p+}∩∂X\{p^{-},p^{+}\}\cap{\partial}Xpointwise.

## Proof.

In this proof,uniformquantities are those bounded in terms of the HHS parameters andMM. Recall thatX=HM,z0​(p−,p+)X=H_{M,z_{0}}(p^{-},p^{+})for allz0∈Xz_{0}\in X, by hypothesis. We first deduce from this that each𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uis uniformly quasi-isometric to an interval, line, or ray. By the preceding observation, it suffices to show that eachhullU,z0⁡(p−,p+)\operatorname{hull}_{U,z_{0}}(p^{-},p^{+})has this property.
Ifp−,p+∈Xp^{-},p^{+}\in X, then by Remark2.8,𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uis at uniformly bounded Hausdorff distance from some geodesic joiningπU​(p−)\pi_{U}(p^{-})toπU​(p+)\pi_{U}(p^{+}), as required.

Therefore, suppose thatp+∈∂Xp^{+}\in{\partial}X. Ifp+∈∂Xp^{+}\in{\partial}Xbutp−∈Xp^{-}\in X, then for allUU,𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uis, by the second point in Definition2.8, at uniformly bounded Hausdorff distance from a geodesic interval or ray, as required. Ifp−,p+∈∂Xp^{-},p^{+}\in{\partial}X, then the third point in Definition2.8yields the same conclusion. (IfU∈supp⁡(p−)∩supp⁡(p+)U\in\operatorname{supp}(p^{-})\cap\operatorname{supp}(p^{+}), we implicitly used thatπU​(p−)≠πU​(p+)\pi_{U}(p^{-})\neq\pi_{U}(p^{+}), but this follows from Definition2.8sinceX≠∅X\neq\emptysetandX=HM,z0​(p−,p+)X=H_{M,z_{0}}(p^{-},p^{+}).)

We now constructTUT_{U}andfUf_{U}for eachU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}. First, we have just shown that there is a uniform constantk1≥1k_{1}\geq 1such that for allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S},𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uis(k1,k1)(k_{1},k_{1})–quasi-isometric to an interval, ray, or line. FixU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}. We defineTUT_{U}andfUf_{U}as follows.

If𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uis two-ended, then Lemma3.1provides a homomorphismϕU:StabG⁡(U)→Isom⁡(ℝ)\phi_{U}:\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(U)\to\operatorname{Isom}(\mathbb{R})and a quasi-isometryfU:𝒞U→ℝ=:TUf_{U}:\mathcal{C}U\to\mathbb{R}=:T_{U}such thatfU​(g​c)=ϕU​(g)​fU​(c)f_{U}(gc)=\phi_{U}(g)f_{U}(c)for allg∈StabG⁡(U)g\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(U)andc∈ℝc\in\mathbb{R}.

Form a new space𝒞′​U\mathcal{C}^{\prime}Uas follows: ifπU​(p+)\pi_{U}(p^{+})(respectively,πU​(p−)\pi_{U}(p^{-})) is contained inXX, then add a vertexv+v^{+}(respectivelyv−v^{-}) joined by an edge to each element ofπU​(p+)\pi_{U}(p^{+})(respectively,πU​(p−)\pi_{U}(p^{-})), and then attach a copy of[0,∞)[0,\infty)by identifying0withv+v^{+}; ifv−v^{-}has been defined, also attach a differente copy of[0,∞)[0,\infty)there. Then𝒞′​U\mathcal{C}^{\prime}Uis a uniform quasiline in which𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uis isometrically embedded as aGG–invariant subset, and theStabG⁡(U)\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(U)action extends naturally over𝒞′​U\mathcal{C}^{\prime}U. Apply Lemma3.1to get aStabG⁡(U)\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(U)–action onℝ\mathbb{R}and an equivariant map𝒞′​U→ℝ\mathcal{C}^{\prime}U\to\mathbb{R}, which we then restrict to𝒞​U\mathcal{C}U.

Applying the above construction toGG–orbit representatives in𝔖\mathfrak{S}and extending equivariantly in the usual way yields isometriesg:TU→Tg​Ug:T_{U}\to T_{gU}with the equivariance properties required by the statement.

Finally, we characterise theU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}for which𝒞​U\mathcal{C}U(equivalentlyTUT_{U}) is unbounded. Ifp±∈∂Xp^{\pm}\in{\partial}XandU∈supp⁡(p±)U\in\operatorname{supp}(p^{\pm}), then𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uis unbounded sinceπU,z0​(p±)∈∂𝒞​U\pi_{U,z_{0}}(p^{\pm})\in{\partial}\mathcal{C}Uin this case. Conversely, suppose that𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uis unbounded. As noted above, this implies that we can assumep+∈∂Xp^{+}\in{\partial}X. SinceGGacts by HHS automorphisms, the set ofU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}for which𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uis unbounded isGG–invariant, which implies thatsupp⁡(p−)∪supp⁡(p+)\operatorname{supp}(p^{-})\cup\operatorname{supp}(p^{+})isGG–invariant (here we takesupp⁡(p±)=∅\operatorname{supp}(p^{\pm})=\emptysetforp±∈Xp^{\pm}\in X). Sincesupp⁡(p±)\operatorname{supp}(p^{\pm})consists of pairwise-orthogonal elements,|supp⁡(p−)∪supp⁡(p+)||\operatorname{supp}(p^{-})\cup\operatorname{supp}(p^{+})|is finite (in fact bounded in terms of the complexity of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})), and henceG′=⋂U∈supp⁡(p−)∪supp⁡(p+)StabG⁡(U)G^{\prime}=\bigcap_{U\in\operatorname{supp}(p^{-})\cup\operatorname{supp}(p^{+})}\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(U)has finite (in fact, uniformly bounded) index inGG. For eachU∈supp⁡(p−)∪supp⁡(p+)U\in\operatorname{supp}(p^{-})\cup\operatorname{supp}(p^{+}), theG′G^{\prime}–action on𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uextends, as usual, to an action on∂𝒞​U{\partial}\mathcal{C}U, which consists of at most two points. Hence passing to the intersections of the kernels of theG′G^{\prime}–actions on these∂𝒞​U{\partial}\mathcal{C}Uyields the desired finite-index subgroup.
∎

sodU​(ρUV,πU​(p+)),dV​(ρVU,πV​(p−))<EordU​(ρUV,πU​(p−)),dV​(ρVU,πV​(p+))<E.d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U},\pi_{U}(p^{+})),d_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V},\pi_{V}(p^{-}))<E\quad\text{or}\quad d_{U}(\rho^{V}_{U},\pi_{U}(p^{-})),d_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V},\pi_{V}(p^{+}))<E.

## Definition\thethm(Clusters, edges and collapsed intervals).

LetU∈𝔘U\in\mathfrak{U}. IfUUis⊑𝔘\sqsubseteq_{\mathfrak{U}}-minimal then letTUc≔∅T_{U}^{c}\coloneqq\emptyset. Otherwise, we say abasic clusterinTUT_{U}is one of the following:

one of the sets{pU−}\{p_{U}^{-}\}or{pU+}\{p_{U}^{+}\}.

Declare two pointst,t′∈TUt,t^{\prime}\in T_{U}to be equivalent if there are basic clustersc,c′c,c^{\prime}such thatdTU​(c,c′)<rd_{T_{U}}(c,c^{\prime})<rand{t,t′}\{t,t^{\prime}\}is contained in the convex hull inTUT_{U}ofc∪c′c\cup c^{\prime}. Extend this to an equivalence relation by taking the transitive closure. AclusterinTUT_{U}is an equivalence class, and we defineTUcT_{U}^{c}to be the union of the clusters.

The components ofTUe≔TU−TUcT_{U}^{e}\coloneqq T_{U}-T_{U}^{c}are callededge components. LetqU:TU→T^Uq_{U}:T_{U}\rightarrow\widehat{T}_{U}be the quotient map which collapses each cluster to a point. LetQ⊆𝒴^Q\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}be the set of consistent tuples and definedQ​(x,y)≔∑U∈𝔘dT^U​(π^U​(x),π^U​(y))d_{Q}(x,y)\coloneqq\sum_{U\in\mathfrak{U}}d_{\widehat{T}_{U}}(\widehat{\pi}_{U}(x),\widehat{\pi}_{U}(y))for allx,y∈Qx,y\in Q.

Remark3.3.(2) and Lemma3.3.(4) imply:

## Lemma\thethm.
- 
- (1)

There existsχ′\chi^{\prime}, depending only onχ\chi, such that, ifx∈Qx\in Qthen for all but at mostχ′\chi^{\prime}domainsU∈𝔘U\in\mathfrak{U}we havexU∈{p^U−,p^U+}x_{U}\in\{\widehat{p}_{U}^{-},\widehat{p}_{U}^{+}\}.

(Q,dQ)(Q,d_{Q})is a median metric space with rank at mostχ\chi.

## Proof.
- 
- (1)

Letx∈Qx\in Q, and let𝔘x≔{V∈𝔘:xV∉{pV±}}\mathfrak{U}_{x}\coloneqq\{V\in\mathfrak{U}:x_{V}\notin\{p_{V}^{\pm}\}\}. IfU,V∈𝔘xU,V\in\mathfrak{U}_{x}thenU⊥VU\bot VorUUandVVare⊑\sqsubseteq-related, by consistency ofxxand Lemma3.3. LetΓ\Gammabe the complete graph with vertex set𝔘x\mathfrak{U}_{x}. Blue cliques have at mostχ\chivertices by the definition ofχ\chiand red cliques have at mostχ\chivertices by[BHS19, Lemma 2.1]. Thus Ramsey’s theorem provides the requiredχ′\chi^{\prime}.
- (2)

Ifp+,p−∈Xp^{+},p^{-}\in XthenRelQ⁡(x^,y^)⊆𝔘⊆RelK/L−L⁡(p+,p−)\operatorname{Rel}_{Q}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y})\subseteq\mathfrak{U}\subseteq\operatorname{Rel}_{K/L-L}(p^{+},p^{-}), which is finite by[CRHK24, Lemma 13.5]and the choice ofKK.

Otherwise, suppose, without loss of generality, thatp+∈∂Xp^{+}\in\partial X. For eachU∈𝔘U\in\mathfrak{U}, letxU∈𝒞​Ux_{U}\in\mathcal{C}Ube such thatdTU​(fU​(xU),x^U)≤Ld_{T_{U}}(f_{U}(x_{U}),\widehat{x}_{U})\leq L, and defineyUy_{U}analogously. Then there is a uniformly bounded neighbourhood ofhull𝒞​V⁡(xV,yV)\operatorname{hull}_{\mathcal{C}V}(x_{V},y_{V})which containsρVU\rho^{U}_{V}for allU∈WVU\in W_{V}. Therefore there exists a pointzV+∈𝒞​Vz_{V}^{+}\in\mathcal{C}Vsuch thatzV+z_{V}^{+}is in the same connected component of𝒞​V−𝒩2​E+MorseE​(1,20​E)​(ρVU)\mathcal{C}V-\mathcal{N}_{2E+\textup{{Morse}}_{E}(1,20E)}(\rho^{U}_{V})asπV​(p+)\pi_{V}(p^{+})for allU∈WVU\in W_{V}. If not, then we can define a pointz−∈Xz^{-}\in Xanalogously toz+z^{+}so thatRelQ⁡(x^,y^)⊆RelK/L+L⁡(z+,z−)\operatorname{Rel}_{Q}(\widehat{x},\widehat{y})\subseteq\operatorname{Rel}_{K/L+L}(z^{+},z^{-}). ## Lemma\thethm.

QQis complete.

## Proof.

Let(xn)n∈ℕ(x^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}be a Cauchy sequence inQQ. For eachU∈𝔘U\in\mathfrak{U}, the sequence(xUn)n∈ℕ⊆T^U(x^{n}_{U})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\widehat{T}_{U}is Cauchy. LetxU≔limn→∞xUnx_{U}\coloneqq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}x^{n}_{U}and letx≔(xU)U∈𝔘x\coloneqq(x_{U})_{U\in\mathfrak{U}}. It follows from the consistency of thexnx^{n}’s thatxxis consistent. To conclude thatlimn→∞xn=x\lim_{n\to\infty}x^{n}=x, it then suffices to show that|∪n∈ℕRelQ⁡(x1,xn)|<∞|\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\operatorname{Rel}_{Q}(x^{1},x^{n})|<\infty. ## Lemma\thethm.

The space(Q,dQ)(Q,d_{Q})isometrically embeds in(ℝk,ℓ1)(\mathbb{R}^{k},\ell_{1})for somekk, and hence the space(Q,dQ)(Q,d_{Q})is proper.

## Proof.

GivenU,V∈𝔘U,V\in\mathfrak{U}, writeU<VU<VifU⋔VU\pitchfork Vandδ^VU=p^V−\widehat{\delta}^{U}_{V}=\widehat{p}_{V}^{-}(and henceδ^UV=p^U+\widehat{\delta}^{V}_{U}=\widehat{p}_{U}^{+}, by Lemma3.3). Note that<<is a partial order on𝔘\mathfrak{U}, andU,V∈𝔘U,V\in\mathfrak{U}are incomparable in this order if and only if they are either orthogonal or⋤\sqsubsetneq–related. Hence, by Ramsey’s theorem, there is a bound (depending only on the complexity of𝔖\mathfrak{S}) on the cardinalities of<<–antichains in𝔘\mathfrak{U}. Therefore, by Dilworth’s theorem[Dil50], there exists a finite partition𝔘=⨆i=1k𝔘i\mathfrak{U}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{U}^{i}where each𝔘i\mathfrak{U}^{i}is a<<–chain. For eachU∈𝔘iU\in\mathfrak{U}^{i}, choosex​(U)∈Qx(U)\in Qsuch thatxU∉{p^U±}x_{U}\not\in\{\hat{p}_{U}^{\pm}\}U∈𝔘x​(U)U\in\mathfrak{U}_{x(U)}, using Lemma3.4.

By Lemma3.4.(2),RelQ⁡(x​(U),x​(V))\operatorname{Rel}_{Q}(x(U),x(V))is finite for allU,V∈𝔘iU,V\in\mathfrak{U}^{i}, so the totally ordered set(𝔘i,<)(\mathfrak{U}^{i},<)has finite intervals, so there is an intervalI⊆ℝI\subseteq\mathbb{R}such that𝔘i={Un:n∈ℤ∩I}\mathfrak{U}^{i}=\{U^{n}:n\in\mathbb{Z}\cap I\}, where theUnU^{n}are labelled so thatn<mn<mif and only ifUn<UmU^{n}<U^{m}. Note that, ifUnU^{n}has a successor (predecessor), then the intervalT^Un\widehat{T}_{U^{n}}has a maximum (minimum). Hence there is a spaceLiL^{i}, isometric toℝ,[0,∞),\mathbb{R},[0,\infty),or a bounded interval, equipped with isometric embeddingsιn:T^Un→Li\iota^{n}:\widehat{T}_{U^{n}}\to L^{i}such that⋃nιn​(T^Un)=Li\bigcup_{n}\iota^{n}(\widehat{T}_{U^{n}})=L^{i}, andιm,ιn\iota^{m},\iota^{n}have disjoint images unless|m−n|≤1|m-n|\leq 1, andιn​(T^Un)∩ιn+1​(T^Un+1)=ιn​(pUn+)=ιn+1​(pUn+1−)\iota^{n}(\widehat{T}_{U^{n}})\cap\iota^{n+1}(\widehat{T}_{U^{n+1}})=\iota^{n}(p_{U^{n}}^{+})=\iota^{n+1}(p_{U^{n+1}}^{-}). Defineπi:Q→Li\pi^{i}:Q\to L^{i}as follows. Givenx∈Qx\in Q, letnnbe maximal such thatxUn=pUn+x_{U^{n}}=p_{U^{n}}^{+}. Ifn+1∉In+1\not\in I, thenLiL^{i}has a maximum, which we take asπi​(x)\pi^{i}(x). Ifn+1n+1exists, we letπi​(x)=ιn+1​(xUn+1)\pi^{i}(x)=\iota^{n+1}(x_{U^{n+1}}). If there is no suchnn, then there is a unique minimaln∈I∩ℤn\in I\cap\mathbb{Z}, and we takeπi​(x)=ιn​(xUn)\pi^{i}(x)=\iota^{n}(x_{U^{n}}). Hence∏i=1kπi:Q→∏i=1kLi\prod_{i=1}^{k}\pi^{i}:Q\to\prod_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}is an isometric embedding (where the codomain has theℓ1\ell_{1}metric), soQQis locally compact and hence, since it is complete by Lemma3.4, proper.

## 3.5.Defining the mapΨ^:X→Q\widehat{\Psi}:X\rightarrow Q

For eachU∈𝔘U\in\mathfrak{U}, letψU≔fU∘πU\psi_{U}\coloneqq f_{U}\circ\pi_{U}. Then defineΨ:X→𝒴\Psi:X\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}byΨ​(x)≔(ψU​(x))U∈𝔘\Psi(x)\coloneqq(\psi_{U}(x))_{U\in\mathfrak{U}}. By Definition2.3and Proposition3.1, the mapΨ\PsiisGG-equivariant.

## Definition\thethm.

Letα≥0\alpha\geq 0. An elementx=(xU)U∈𝔘∈𝒴x=(x_{U})_{U\in\mathfrak{U}}\in\mathcal{Y}isα\alpha-consistentif, for allU,V∈𝔘U,V\in\mathfrak{U},

ifU⋔VU\pitchfork Vthenmin⁡{dTU​(xU,δUV),dTV​(xV,δVU)}≤α\min\{d_{T_{U}}(x_{U},\delta^{V}_{U}),d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V})\}\leq\alpha;
- •

ifU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq Vthenmin⁡{dTV​(xV,δVU),diam⁡({xU}∪δUV​(xV))}≤α\min\{d_{T_{V}}(x_{V},\delta^{U}_{V}),\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\{x_{U}\}\cup\delta^{V}_{U}(x_{V}))\}\leq\alpha.

Let𝒵α\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha}be the set ofα\alpha-consistent tuples in𝒴\mathcal{Y}.

Next is an elementary consequence of Lemma3.2and the HHS consistency axioms:

## Lemma\thethm.

By construction,Ψ^\widehat{\Psi}isGG–equivariant.

## Lemma\thethm.

The image ofΨ^\widehat{\Psi}is consistent in the sense of Definition3.4, i.e.Ψ^​(X)⊆Q\widehat{\Psi}(X)\subseteq Q.

## Proof.

Letx∈Xx\in XandU,V∈𝔘U,V\in\mathfrak{U}. IfU⋔VU\pitchfork Vthenmin⁡{dTU​(ψU​(x),δUV),dTV​(ψV​(x),δVU)}≤E′<R\min\{d_{T_{U}}(\psi_{U}(x),\delta^{V}_{U}),d_{T_{V}}(\psi_{V}(x),\delta^{U}_{V})\}\leq E^{\prime}<Rso eitherψ^U​(x)=δ^UV\widehat{\psi}_{U}(x)=\widehat{\delta}^{V}_{U}orψ^V​(x)=δ^VU\widehat{\psi}_{V}(x)=\widehat{\delta}^{U}_{V}. SupposeU⋤VU\sqsubsetneq V. Ifψ^V​(x)≠δ^VU\widehat{\psi}_{V}(x)\neq\widehat{\delta}^{U}_{V}, thendTV​(δVU,ψV​(x))>R>50​E′d_{T_{V}}(\delta^{U}_{V},\psi_{V}(x))>R>50E^{\prime}sodV​(ρVU,x)>50​Ed_{V}(\rho^{U}_{V},x)>50E. Thereforediam𝒞​U⁡(πU​(x)∪ρUV​(πV​(x)))≤E\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}_{\mathcal{C}U}(\pi_{U}(x)\cup\rho^{V}_{U}(\pi_{V}(x)))\leq Eby the consistency axiom for HHSes ([BHS19, Defn. 1.1.(4)]). It follows from the bounded geodesic image axiom for HHSes ([BHS19, Defn. 1.1.(7)]) thatmin⁡{diam𝒞​U⁡(ρUV​(πV​(x))∪πU​(p−)),diam𝒞​U⁡(ρUV​(πV​(x))∪πU​(p+))}≤5​E,\min\{\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}_{\mathcal{C}U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\pi_{V}(x))\cup\pi_{U}(p^{-})),\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}_{\mathcal{C}U}(\rho^{V}_{U}(\pi_{V}(x))\cup\pi_{U}(p^{+}))\}\leq 5E,

soψU​(x)=pU+\psi_{U}(x)=p_{U}^{+}orpU−p_{U}^{-}andψU​(x)∈δUV​(ψV​(x))\psi_{U}(x)\in\delta^{V}_{U}(\psi_{V}(x)). Thusψ^U​(x)∈δ^UV​(ψ^V​(x))\widehat{\psi}_{U}(x)\in\widehat{\delta}^{V}_{U}(\widehat{\psi}_{V}(x)), as required.

IfxV=pV−x_{V}=p_{V}^{-}then letCV≔C∩hullTU⁡({pU−}∪𝒩R​(δUV))C_{V}\coloneqq C\cap\operatorname{hull}_{T_{U}}(\{p_{U}^{-}\}\cup\mathcal{N}_{R}(\delta^{V}_{U}))and ifxV=pV+x_{V}=p_{V}^{+}then letCV≔C∩hullTU⁡(𝒩R​(δUV)∪{pU+})C_{V}\coloneqq C\cap\operatorname{hull}_{T_{U}}(\mathcal{N}_{R}(\delta^{V}_{U})\cup\{p_{U}^{+}\}).

## Claim 1.

IdentifyTUT_{U}isometrically with a subset ofℝ\mathbb{R}such that, ifpU+,pU−∈TUp_{U}^{+},p_{U}^{-}\in T_{U}thenpU−<pU+p_{U}^{-}<p_{U}^{+}and, ifpU+∈∂TUp_{U}^{+}\in\partial T_{U}(resp.pU−∈∂TUp_{U}^{-}\in\partial T_{U}) then, extending the identification to the boundary,pU+=+∞p_{U}^{+}=+\infty(resp.pU−=−∞p_{U}^{-}=-\infty).

We definexU∈TUx_{U}\in T_{U}as follows, distinguishing two cases. First, ifU∉supp⁡(p−)∪supp⁡(p+)U\not\in\operatorname{supp}(p^{-})\cup\operatorname{supp}(p^{+}), thenTUT_{U}is a finite interval. ## 4.HHS actions with bounded orbits

The purpose of this section is to prove:

## Proposition\thethm.

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS. Then there existsk0k_{0}, depending only on the HHS parameters, such that for allk1≥k0​(2​k0+1)k_{1}\geq k_{0}(2k_{0}+1), there existk2,…,k7k_{2},\dots,k_{7}, depending only onk1k_{1}and the HHS parameters, andT′T^{\prime}depending only onk1,…,k4k_{1},\ldots,k_{4}and the HHS parameters, such that the following holds.

Z≠∅Z\neq\emptyset;

there exists a non-empty injective spaceYY, equipped with the trivialGGaction, and aGG–equivariant(k2,k2)(k_{2},k_{2})–quasi-isometryq:Z→Yq:Z\to Y, whereZZhas the subspace metric inherited fromXX;

there is an unbounded, increasing functionω0:[0,∞)→[0,∞)\omega_{0}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty), depending only on the HHS parameters, such thatdiamX⁡(G⋅x)≥ω0​(d​(x,Z))\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}_{X}(G\cdot x)\geq\omega_{0}(d(x,Z))for allx∈Xx\in X;

ifG0⊴GG_{0}\unlhd Ghas the property thatg​V⊬VgV\>\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{90.0}{$\nvdash$}}Vfor allV∈𝔖V\in\mathfrak{S}such thatπV​(Z)\pi_{V}(Z)has diameter at leastT′T^{\prime}, thenHθ​(Z)⊆Z​(G0,X,k7)H_{\theta}(Z)\subseteq Z(G_{0},X,k_{7}). This holds in particular ifG0G_{0}is contained in the intersection of all subgroups ofGGwith index≤χ!\leq\chi!.

Finally, suppose that there existsBBsuch thatdiam⁡(𝒞​U)≤B\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\mathcal{C}U)\leq Bordiam⁡(𝒞​U)=∞\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(\mathcal{C}U)=\inftyfor allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}. Then one can replaceχ\chiin item (6) with the maximalrrsuch that𝔖\mathfrak{S}contains a pairwise-orthogonal set{U1,…,Ur}\{U_{1},\ldots,U_{r}\}with each𝒞​Ui\mathcal{C}U_{i}unbounded.

## Proof.

In view of Lemma2.12, TheoremA.4and Corollary2.11, we can and shall assume thatXXhas been chosen in itsGG–equivariant quasi-isometry class so that there is a constantk0k_{0}, depending only on the HHS parameters, and aGG–equivariant,k0k_{0}–coarsely surjective,(k0,k0)(k_{0},k_{0})–quasi-isometryι:X→Env​(X)\iota:X\to\mathrm{Env}(X), whereEnv​(X)\mathrm{Env}(X)is the injective hull ofXX.

Construction ofYY.SinceGGacts onEnv​(X)\mathrm{Env}(X)with bounded orbits, the setY⊆Env​(X)Y\subseteq\mathrm{Env}(X)of points fixed byGGis nonempty and injective by[Lan13, Prop. 1.2]. HenceZ0:=ι−1​(𝒩k0​(Y))Z_{0}:=\iota^{-1}(\mathcal{N}_{k_{0}}(Y))is a nonempty,GG–invariant subset ofXX. The same computation as in the proof of Corollary2.11shows thatd​(x,g​x)≤k0​(2​k0+1)d(x,gx)\leq k_{0}(2k_{0}+1)for allx∈Z0x\in Z_{0}.

## Claim 1.

There is an unbounded, increasing functiont:[0,∞)→[0,∞)t:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty), depending only on the HHS parameters, such thatdiamX⁡(G⋅x)≥t​(d​(x,Z0))\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}_{X}(G\cdot x)\geq t(d(x,Z_{0}))for allx∈Xx\in X.

## Proof of Claim1.

Letx∈Env​(X)x\in\mathrm{Env}(X)and letR=diamEnv​(X)⁡(G⋅x)R=\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}_{\mathrm{Env}(X)}(G\cdot x). Proposition 1.2 of[Lan13], applied to this subspace, shows thatdEnv​(X)​(Y,G⋅x)≤Rd_{\mathrm{Env}(X)}(Y,G\cdot x)\leq R, sodEnv​(X)​(Y,x)≤2​Rd_{\mathrm{Env}(X)}(Y,x)\leq 2R. In particular, ifz∈Xz\in X, thendEnv​(X)​(ι​(z),Y)≤2​diamEnv​(X)⁡(G⋅ι​(z))d_{\mathrm{Env}(X)}(\iota(z),Y)\leq 2\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}_{\mathrm{Env}(X)}(G\cdot\iota(z)), which implies the claim sinceι\iotais an equivariant,k0k_{0}–coarsely surjective,(k0,k0)(k_{0},k_{0})–quasi-isometry.

Letk1≥k0​(2​k0+1)k_{1}\geq k_{0}(2k_{0}+1)be given, and letZ=Z​(G,X,k1)Z=Z(G,X,k_{1})be as in the statement.

## Claim 2.

The setZZis nonempty andGG–invariant, and the Hausdorff distance inXXbetweenZZandZ0Z_{0}is bounded above in terms ofk0k_{0}andk1k_{1}.

## Proof of Claim2.

ThatZZisGG–invariant is immediate from the definition. Sincez∈Z0z\in Z_{0}impliesdiam⁡(G⋅z)≤k0​(2​k0+1)\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(G\cdot z)\leq k_{0}(2k_{0}+1), we haveZ0⊆ZZ_{0}\subseteq Z; in particular,Z≠∅Z\neq\emptyset. Letttbe as in Claim1. ThenZ⊆𝒩t−1​(k1)​(Z0)Z\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{t^{-1}(k_{1})}(Z_{0}), yielding the required Hausdorff distance bound.

Item (5) follows immediately from Claims1and2. SinceYYis injective — and therefore an absolute 1-lipschitz retract (see e.g.[Lan13, Prop. 2.2]) — and an isometrically embedded subspace ofEnv​(X)\mathrm{Env}(X), there is a 1-lipschitz retractionEnv​(X)→Y\mathrm{Env}(X)\rightarrow Y. Using Claim2and the fact thatι\iotais(k0,k0)(k_{0},k_{0})–quasi-isometry, we can therefore compose uniformly coarsely lipschitz mapsX→Env​(X)→Y→Z0→ZX\rightarrow\mathrm{Env}(X)\rightarrow Y\rightarrow Z_{0}\rightarrow Zto produce a coarsely lipschitz mapρ:X→Z\rho:X\rightarrow Z. Up to uniformly perturbingρ\rho(here, uniformity allows dependence onk0,k1k_{0},k_{1}, and the HHS parameters, as in the statement), we can assume it is the identity onZZ, proving Item (4).

## 5.Some invariant subspaces

The next definition abstracts a property of the action by HHS automorphisms of an HHG on itself (see Definition2.3) by[DHS20, Thm. 3.1].

## Definition\thethm(Hierarchically semisimple).

An action of a groupHHon an HHS(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})by HHS automorphisms ishierarchically semisimpleif, for allg∈Hg\in Hand allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}such thatg​U=UgU=Uand⟨g⟩\langle g\ranglehas unbounded orbits in𝒞​U\mathcal{C}U, the isometryggof𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uis loxodromic.
∎

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS and letA≤Isom⁡(X)A\leq\operatorname{Isom}(X)be an infinite finitely generated virtually abelian group acting hierarchically semisimply on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})by HHS automorphisms, with theAA–orbits inXXunbounded. The goal of this section is to analyse some canonicalAA-invariant HQC and EHQC subspaces, using the results of the previous sections.

By Lemma2.12and Lemma2.12, we can assume thatXXis a graph and theAA–action onXXis free. For convenience, up to a uniform enlargement of the HHS parameters, we can and shall assume that(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})isnormalisedin the sense of[BHS19, Rem. 1.3], which is to say thatπU\pi_{U}isEE–coarsely surjective for allU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}. Recall thatχ\chiis the complexity of𝔖\mathfrak{S}.

for allU∈supp⁡(p±)U\in\operatorname{supp}(p^{\pm})we haveπU​(p+)≠πU​(p−)\pi_{U}(p^{+})\neq\pi_{U}(p^{-});

for anyx∈Xx\in X, the projectionπU​(A⋅x)\pi_{U}(A\cdot x)is unbounded if and only ifU∈supp⁡(p±)U\in\operatorname{supp}(p^{\pm}).

AApreserves the set{Ui}i=1n\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}.

LetA˙˙˙≤A\dddot{A}\leq Abe the kernel of theAA–action on{Ui}i=1n\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}; note that[A:A˙˙˙]≤χ![A:\dddot{A}]\leq\chi!. LetA¨≤A˙˙˙\ddot{A}\leq\dddot{A}be a finite index abelian subgroup.

Combined with the hierarchical semisimplicity assumption, this implies that somebi∈A¨b_{i}\in\ddot{A}acts on𝒞​Ui\mathcal{C}U_{i}loxodromically, for eachi∈{1,…,n}i\in\{1,\dots,n\}. The classification of actions on hyperbolic spaces (see, for instance,[CCMT15, Prop. 3.1]) therefore allows three possibilities for theA¨\ddot{A}–action on𝒞​Ui\mathcal{C}U_{i}: it can belineal, focal,orgeneral-type. LetA′≤A¨A^{\prime}\leq\ddot{A}be a further finite index subgroup which fixes eachpi±p_{i}^{\pm}. Then Items (1)–(4) all hold.

## Proof.

We start with a claim which will help to establish theAA–invariance of the various subspaces we are interested in.

## Claim 1.

## Proof of Claim1.

It follows thata​γV=γa​Va\gamma_{V}=\gamma_{aV}for allV∈𝔖−supp⟂⁡(p±)V\in\mathfrak{S}-\operatorname{supp}^{\perp}(p^{\pm}).

We now prepare to use the realisation theorem to findkk. Sincec​(X​(A))c(X(A))is uniformly EHQC inPˇ\widecheck{P}andHHis uniformly HQC inPP, it also follows from this thatX​(A)X(A)is uniformly EHQC. ## Lemma\thethm.

SupposeA≤BA\leq B, and the action ofAAextends to an action ofBBonXXby HHS automorphisms. Then the following hold:

- (3)

IfAAis normal inBB, thenBBstabilisesX​(A)X(A).

## Proof.

First assume thatCommB⁡(A)=B\operatorname{Comm}_{B}({A})=B. LetV∈𝔖V\in\mathfrak{S}andx∈Xx\in X. ThenπV​(A⋅x)\pi_{V}(A\cdot x)is unbounded if and only ifV∈supp⁡(p+)V\in\operatorname{supp}(p^{+}), by Lemma5.(4). The same holds whenAAis replaced by any finite-index subgroup. Thus there is a finite-indexB′′≤BB^{\prime\prime}\leq Bthat fixes each of the finitely manyU∈supp⁡(p+)U\in\operatorname{supp}(p^{+}).

So, up to passing to a further finite-index subgroup,B′′B^{\prime\prime}fixesp−p^{-}andp+p^{+}, proving (1). Sincesupp⁡(p±)\operatorname{supp}(p^{\pm})isBB–invariant, andBBpreserves the⊑,⊥,⋔\sqsubseteq,\bot,\pitchforkrelations in𝔖\mathfrak{S}, the set𝔘\mathfrak{U}isBB–invariant, which implies item (2), using Corollary2.10. This argument also shows that theBB–action on∏V∈𝔘𝒞​V\prod_{V\in\mathfrak{U}}\mathcal{C}Vpreserves the subset∏V∈𝔘γV\prod_{V\in\mathfrak{U}}\gamma_{V}.

Now assumeA⊴BA\unlhd B, so items (1) and (2) apply andBBstabilisesZˇ\widecheck{Z}.

## 6.Hierarchical flat torus theorem

Now we can state and prove the main theorem:{thm}

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS of complexityχ\chi. Then there exist constantsp,qp,qandrr, depending only on the HHS parameters, such that the following holds. LetA≤Isom⁡(X)A\leq\operatorname{Isom}(X)be a finitely-generated, infinite, virtually abelian group acting properly and hierarchically semisimply by HHS automorphisms on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}). Then:

- (3)

There is an injective uniformly coarse median metric space(Y,dY)(Y,d_{Y})and a CAT(0) space(C,dC)(C,d_{C})with the following properties. LetAAact onY×C×𝔼nY\times C\times\mathbb{E}^{n}, trivially on the first and second factors and viaα\alphaon the third. Then there is a(p,q)(p,q)–EHQC,AA–invariantrr–coarse lipschitz retractℳA⊆X\mathcal{M}_{A}\subseteq Xand anAA–equivariant(p,p)(p,p)–quasi-isometryΘ:ℳA→Y×C×𝔼n,\Theta:\mathcal{M}_{A}\to Y\times C\times\mathbb{E}^{n},

whereℳA\mathcal{M}_{A}has the metric inherited fromXX. Moreover, for each(y,c)∈Y×C(y,c)\in Y\times C, theAA–invariant subset F_(y,c):=Θ^-1(N_k((y,c))×𝔼\mathbb{E}^n) is(p,q)(p,q)–EHQC and anrr–coarse lipschitz retract.
- (4)

There existsrAr_{A}, depending onAA, and a free abelian subgroupA0⊴AA_{0}\unlhd Asuch thatdH​a​u​s​(ℳA0,X​(A0))≤rAd_{Haus}(\mathcal{M}_{A_{0}},X(A_{0}))\leq r_{A}. - (5)

We used the following standard lemmas in the above proof.

## Lemma\thethm.

LetDDbe a CAT(0) space, letGGbe a group acting elliptically onDDand letFix⁡(G)⊆D\operatorname{Fix}(G)\subseteq Dbe the fixed point set ofGG. Thendiam⁡(G⋅x)≥d​(x,Fix⁡(G))\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(G\cdot x)\geq d(x,\operatorname{Fix}(G))for allx∈Dx\in D.

## Proof.

Fixx∈Dx\in D. Letrrbe the infimal radius of balls containing the bounded setG⋅xG\cdot x; note thatr≤diam⁡(G⋅x)r\leq\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(G\cdot x). Then[BH99, Prop. II.2.7]provides a unique circumcentre forG⋅xG\cdot x, i.e. a pointc∈Dc\in Dsuch thatG⋅x⊂𝒩r​(c)G\cdot x\subset\mathcal{N}_{r}(c). Sinceg⋅cg\cdot chas this property for allg∈Gg\in G, uniqueness ofccimplies thatc∈Fix⁡(G)c\in\operatorname{Fix}(G), sod​(x,Fix⁡(G))≤d​(x,c)≤r≤diam⁡(G⋅x)d(x,\operatorname{Fix}(G))\leq d(x,c)\leq r\leq\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(G\cdot x).
∎

## Lemma\thethm.

For eachn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}, there is a functionC′:[1,∞)→[0,∞)C^{\prime}:[1,\infty)\to[0,\infty)such that the following holds. Letf:𝔼n→𝔼nf:\mathbb{E}^{n}\to\mathbb{E}^{n}be a(C,C)(C,C)–coarsely lipschitz map. Suppose thatsupx∈𝔼ndiam⁡(f−1​(𝒩r​(x)))<∞\sup_{x\in\mathbb{E}^{n}}\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(f^{-1}(\mathcal{N}_{r}(x)))<\inftyfor allr≥0r\geq 0. ThenffisC′​(C)C^{\prime}(C)–coarsely surjective.

## Proof.

By a standard triangulation argument, there is a continuous mapg:𝔼n→𝔼ng:\mathbb{E}^{n}\to\mathbb{E}^{n}such thatsupx∈𝔼n|f​(x)−g​(x)|≤R\sup_{x\in\mathbb{E}^{n}}|f(x)-g(x)|\leq R, andggis(K,K)(K,K)–coarsely lipschitz, for someR,KR,Kdepending only onCC. We will show thatggis surjective, from which it will follow thatffisRR–coarsely surjective.

LetE¯\bar{E}be the one-point compactification of𝔼n\mathbb{E}^{n}. Sinceggis continuous and preimages of bounded sets are bounded,ggis proper map. Henceggextends to a continuous mapg¯:E¯→E¯\bar{g}:\bar{E}\to\bar{E}withg¯​(∞)=∞\bar{g}(\infty)=\infty.

Define a homeomorphismh:E¯→𝕊nh:\bar{E}\to\mathbb{S}^{n}to be stereographic projection, where𝔼n\mathbb{E}^{n}is viewed as a coordinate plane in𝔼n+1\mathbb{E}^{n+1}and𝕊n\mathbb{S}^{n}is viewed as the sphere in𝔼n+1\mathbb{E}^{n+1}centred at the origin and with radiusRR, whereR=supx∈𝔼ndiam⁡(g−1​(𝒩1​(x)))<∞R=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{E}^{n}}\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}(g^{-1}(\mathcal{N}_{1}(x)))<\infty. The “north pole” for the projection is in the direction normal to𝔼n\mathbb{E}^{n}, and𝕊n\mathbb{S}^{n}is identified withE¯\bar{E}in such a way that the north pole is∞∈E¯\infty\in\bar{E}.

This choice ofhhhas the following property: ifx,y∈𝔼nx,y\in\mathbb{E}^{n}andg​(x)=g​(y)g(x)=g(y), thenh​(x)h(x)andh​(y)h(y)are not antipodal.

Ifggwere not surjective, thenh∘g¯∘h−1:𝕊n→𝕊nh\circ\bar{g}\circ h^{-1}:\mathbb{S}^{n}\to\mathbb{S}^{n}has range properly contained in𝕊n\mathbb{S}^{n}, to the Borsuk-Ulam theorem provides antipodal pointsh​(x),h​(y)∈𝕊nh(x),h(y)\in\mathbb{S}^{n}such thatg¯​(x)=g¯​(y)\bar{g}(x)=\bar{g}(y). Sinceg¯−1​(∞)\bar{g}^{-1}(\infty)has a single point,x,y∈𝔼nx,y\in\mathbb{E}^{n}andg​(x)=g​(y)g(x)=g(y), a contradiction.
∎

Moreover,ϕx​(A)\phi_{x}(A)is contained in the(k,r)(k,r)–EHQC,AA–invariant(k,k)(k,k)–quasiflatFFfrom Theorem6.(3).

Let𝐄=𝔼n×Ω\mathbf{E}=\mathbb{E}^{n}\times\Omega, so that the diagonalAA–action on𝐄\mathbf{E}is free, metrically proper, and cobounded. LetdAd_{A}be the metric onAAobtained by pulling back the subspace metric on the orbitA⋅(0→,1A)⊂𝐄A\cdot(\vec{0},1_{A})\subset\mathbf{E}. Note thatdAd_{A}is uniformly quasi-isometric to the pseudometric coming from the orbit mapA→𝔼nA\to\mathbb{E}^{n}given bya↦α​(a)⋅0→a\mapsto\alpha(a)\cdot\vec{0}.

We can assume thatq​(x)=0→q(x)=\vec{0}. Defineϕx:(A,dA)→(Fx,dX)\phi_{x}:(A,d_{A})\to(F_{x},d_{X})byϕx​(a)=a​x\phi_{x}(a)=ax. Thenq∘ϕx​(a)=α​(a)⋅0→q\circ\phi_{x}(a)=\alpha(a)\cdot\vec{0}, which is to say thatq∘ϕxq\circ\phi_{x}is the orbit map associated toα\alpha, which is, by construction, a quasi-isometry with uniform constants. Sinceqqis a quasi-isometry with constants independent ofxxandAA, it follows thatϕx\phi_{x}is also.

## 7.Applications

We now describe some applications to groups acting on HHSes.

## Definition\thethm.

LetΓ\Gammabe a group and letA≤ΓA\leq\Gammabe a virtually abelian subgroup. ThenAAishighestif for all finite-index subgroupsA′≤AA^{\prime}\leq Aand all virtually abelian subgroupsA′′≤GA^{\prime\prime}\leq GwithA′≤A′′A^{\prime}\leq A^{\prime\prime}, we have[A′′:A′]<∞[A^{\prime\prime}:A^{\prime}]<\infty.
∎

## Lemma\thethm.

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS and supposeGGacts uniformly properly and hierarchically semisimply by isometric HHS automorphisms on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}). Then there are only finitely many abstract isomorphism types of finitely generated virtually abelian subgroups ofGG. In particular, there are constantsB,N0,R0B,N_{0},R_{0}, depending onGGbut not on the specific choice of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}), such that for all finitely generated virtually abelianA≤GA\leq G,
- •

|A|≤B|A|\leq Bif|A|<∞|A|<\infty;
- •

there is a free abelian subgroupA0≤AA_{0}\leq Awith[A:A0]≤N0[A:A_{0}]\leq N_{0};
- •

rk⁡(A)≤R0\operatorname{rk}(A)\leq R_{0}.

## Proof.

[store=cor:solvable] 
Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS and letGGbe a group acting uniformly properly and hierarchically semisimply by HHS automorphisms onXX. IfGGis virtually solvable thenGGis virtually abelian.

## Proof.

SupposeGGis virtually solvable with derived lengthk≥1k\geq 1. We proceed by induction onkk. Ifk=1k=1thenGGis abelian, so supposek>1k>1andG′≔[G,G]G^{\prime}\coloneqq[G,G]is virtually abelian. Moreover the element ofH2​(H/A,A)H^{2}(H/A,A)corresponding to the central extension1→A→H→H/A→11\rightarrow A\rightarrow H\rightarrow H/A\rightarrow 1is bounded. This implies thatHHis quasi-isometric toA×H/AA\times H/A, by[Ger92]. Letπ:H→H/A\pi:H\rightarrow H/Abe the quotient map and letH′≔[H,H]H^{\prime}\coloneqq[H,H]. SinceH′≤G′H^{\prime}\leq G^{\prime}, the imageπ​(H′)\pi(H^{\prime})is finite. Moreoverπ​(H′)\pi(H^{\prime})is normal inH/AH/Aand(H/A)/π​(H′)=H/H′(H/A)/\pi(H^{\prime})=H/H^{\prime}is abelian, so[BH99, II.7.9]implies thatH/AH/Ais virtually abelian. ThereforeA×H/AA\times H/Ais virtually abelian and, since virtually abelian groups are quasi-isometrically rigid[Pan83](see[Dru09, Sec. 4.3]), this implies thatHHis virtually abelian.

As we remarked earlier, the hierarchical semisimplicity assumption holds for HHGs, so the theorems of Section6apply to HHGs, yielding the following “coarse flat torus” result:{cor}

Let(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S})be an HHG with complexityχ\chi.
There exists a constantkk, depending only on the HHS parameters, such that for any virtually abelian subgroupA≤GA\leq G, there is anAA–invariant(k,k)(k,k)–EHQC subspaceF⊆GF\subseteq G, a proper, cocompact, isometricAA–action on𝔼n\mathbb{E}^{n}for somen∈{0,…​χ}n\in\{0,\ldots\,\chi\}, and anAA–equivariant(k,k)(k,k)–quasi-isometryF→𝔼nF\to\mathbb{E}^{n}.

In particular, virtually abelian subgroups ofGGare finitely generated and belong to one of finitely many abstract isomorphism types.

## Proof.

Hierarchical semisimplicity comes from[DHS20, Thm. 3.1], at which point Theorem6applies, sinceAAis finitely generated by Corollary7. This gives the subspacesFFfrom the statement, while the statement about isomorphism types comes from Lemma7.
∎

## 7.2.Ruling out HHG structures using highest virtually abelian subgroups

Highest virtually abelian subgroups can be used to rule out the existence of HHG structures on a group using the following corollary.{cor}

[store=cor:highest-abelian]
Let(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S})be an HHG. Every highest virtually abelian subgroupA≤GA\leq Gis hierarchically quasiconvex. Thus every abelian subgroup is virtually contained in a hierarchically quasiconvex abelian subgroup.

## Proof.

Suppose thatAAis highest. Corollary7.1provides a finite-index subgroupA1≤AA_{1}\leq Asuch thatCG​(A1)C_{G}(A_{1})is hierarchically quasiconvex. IfCG​(A1)C_{G}(A_{1})has a nonabelian free subgroup, then there existsg∈CG​(A1)g\in C_{G}(A_{1})such that⟨g,A1⟩≅ℤ×A1\langle g,A_{1}\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}\times A_{1}, contradicting thatAAis highest. Hence, by[DHS20, Thm. 4.1], every finitely generated subgroup ofCG​(A1)C_{G}(A_{1})is virtually abelian, so Corollary7implies[CG(A1):A1]<∞[C_{G}(A_{1}):A_{1}]<\infty, soA1A_{1}(and thusAA) is HQC.
∎

Using this, we can describe the possible crystallographic groups that can appear as highest virtually abelian subgroups of HHGs, by generalising[PS23, Thm. 4.4]. Recall from Definition2.13the notion of a hyperoctahedral virtually abelian group.{cor}

Let(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S})be an HHG. LetA≤GA\leq Gbe a highest virtually abelian subgroup. ThenAAis hyperoctahedral.

## Proof.

By Corollary7.2,AAis hierarchically quasiconvex inGG. HenceAAacts properly and coboundedly on an HHS, by Lemma2.6, and is therefore coarsely injective, by Corollary2.11. This, together with[Hod23, Thm. A]implies that the point group is conjugate inG​Ln​(ℝ)GL_{n}(\mathbb{R})into the isometry group of(𝔼n,∥⋅∥∞)(\mathbb{E}^{n},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}), which is to sayOn​(ℤ)O_{n}(\mathbb{Z}).
∎

## Remark\thethm.

One can use highest virtually abelian subgroupsAAof a groupGGas an obstruction to cocompact cubulation ofGG, as follows: ifGGis cocompactly cubulated, thenAAis also cocompactly cubulated, by[WW17, Thm. 3.6], soAAis hyperoctahedral by[Hag14]or[Hod23]. Corollary7.2can be used in an analogous way to rule out the existence of HHG structures in various examples. We now illustrate this in the case of Coxeter groups.
∎

Let(W,S)(W,S)be a Coxeter system. Following[Kra09, Sec. 6], astandard abelian subgroupA≤WA\leq Whas the form∏i=1kHi\prod_{i=1}^{k}H_{i}, where eachHiH_{i}is defined as follows: there are irreducible, non-spherical subsetsI1,…,IkI_{1},\ldots,I_{k}ofSSsuch that⟨WIi,WIj⟩W≅WIi×WIj\langle W_{I_{i}},W_{I_{j}}\rangle_{W}\cong W_{I_{i}}\times W_{I_{j}}fori≠ji\neq j, and, ifIiI_{i}is affine, thenHi≤f.i.WIiH_{i}\leq_{f.i.}W_{I_{i}}is the translation subgroup, and otherwiseHi≅ℤH_{i}\cong\mathbb{Z}. Astandard virtually abelian subgrouphas the form∏iH^i\prod_{i}\widehat{H}_{i}, whereH^i=WIi\widehat{H}_{i}=W_{I_{i}}forIiI_{i}irreducible affine, andH^i=Hi≅ℤ\widehat{H}_{i}=H_{i}\cong\mathbb{Z}otherwise.{cor}

Let(W,S)(W,S)be a Coxeter system. IfWWis a hierarchically hyperbolic group, then every maximal standard virtually abelian subgroup is hyperoctahedral.

## Proof.

By[Kra09, Thm. 6.8.3], every abelian subgroup ofWWis virtually contained in a conjugate of a standard abelian subgroup. Hence maximal standard virtually abelian subgroups are highest virtually abelian subgroups, and the claim follows from Corollary7.2.
∎

For example, ifWWcontains the(3,3,3)(3,3,3)triangle group as a maximal standard virtually abelian subgroup, thenWWis not an HHG.

## Lemma\thethm.

Let(G,𝔖)(G,\mathfrak{S})be a normalised HHG and letU1,…,Uk⊆𝔖U_{1},\dots,U_{k}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}be pairwise orthogonal domains such that, for anyU⋤UiU\sqsubsetneq U_{i}, the space𝒞​U\mathcal{C}Uis bounded. Then the restriction ofπUi\pi_{U_{i}}toQQis10​E10E–coarsely surjective for eachii, and⋂i=1kStabG⁡(Ui)\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(U_{i})acts cocompactly onQQ. Therefore⋂i=1kStabG⁡(Ui)\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\operatorname{Stab}_{G}(U_{i})acts coboundedly on𝒞​Ui\mathcal{C}U_{i}for eachii.

## Proof.

The fact that the restriction of eachπUi\pi_{U_{i}}toQQis10​E10E–coarsely surjective is a consequence of the partial realisation axiom[BHS19, Def. 1.1.(8)]. To prove the cocompactness statement, we will induct onkk.

Ifk=1k=1thenQ=PU1Q=P_{U_{1}}, so cocompactness holds by Lemma7.3. Supposek>1k>1and suppose the claim holds for sets of up tok−1k-1domains. LetT⊆HT\subseteq Hbe a left-transversal forStabH⁡(Uk)\operatorname{Stab}_{H}(U_{k}). LetI⊆TI\subseteq Tbe such that{h​Q:h∈I}\{hQ:h\in I\}all intersect the same ball inQ′Q^{\prime}. For eachh∈Hh\in H, we haveh​Q=h​(Q′∩PUk)=Q′∩Ph​UkhQ=h(Q^{\prime}\cap P_{U_{k}})=Q^{\prime}\cap P_{hU_{k}}, so this implies that{Ph​Uk:h∈I}\{P_{hU_{k}}:h\in I\}all intersect the same ball inGG. The pigeonhole principle for cocompact actions then implies thatStabH⁡(Q)\operatorname{Stab}_{H}(Q)acts cocompactly onQQ. ## Appendix AChasing constants

Throguhout, we used some results from the literature on hierarchical hyperbolicity that take as input an HHS(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})and yield some conclusion involving various quantities depending only on the HHS parameters for(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}). This uniformity of constants is not always explicitly asserted in the versions of those statements in the literature, but can be extracted from their proofs. In this appendix, we make the dependence of the constants explicit in these statements.

## A.1.Strong distance formula

The definition of an HHS is designed to enable one to prove several results that then serve as the main tools; most notable is thedistance formulafrom[BHS19], of which special cases appear in[MM00,Sis13,BHS17a]. In the statement, we usethresholdnotation: givenA,T∈ℝA,T\in\mathbb{R}, let{{A}}T≔A\left\{\kern-3.01385pt\left\{A\right\}\kern-3.01385pt\right\}_{T}\coloneqq AifA≥TA\geq Tand0ifA<TA<T.{thm}

[Strong distance formula]
For allλ≥1\lambda\geq 1, the following holds. Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be a hierarchically hyperbolic space and leth:[0,∞)→[0,∞)h:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)be such thatλ−1​r−λ≤h​(r)≤λ​r+λ\lambda^{-1}r-\lambda\leq h(r)\leq\lambda r+\lambdafor allr≥0r\geq 0. Then there existsT0≥2​λT_{0}\geq 2\lambda, depending only onλ\lambdaand the HHS parameters, such that for allT≥T0T\geq T_{0}, there existsκ≥0\kappa\geq 0, depending only onT,λ,T,\lambda,and the HHS parameters, such thatκ−1​dX​(x,y)−κ≤∑U∈𝔖{{h​(dU​(x,y))}}T≤κ​d​(x,y)+κ\kappa^{-1}d_{X}(x,y)-\kappa\leq\sum_{U\in\mathfrak{S}}\left\{\kern-3.01385pt\left\{h(d_{U}(x,y))\right\}\kern-3.01385pt\right\}_{T}\leq\kappa d(x,y)+\kappa

for allx,y∈Xx,y\in X.

## Proof.

The caseλ=1\lambda=1is the original distance formula for HHSes, which is[BHS19, Thm. 4.5], whose proof makes clear that the thresholdT0T_{0}forλ=1\lambda=1depends only on the HHS parameters and, for anyT≥T0T\geq T_{0}, one obtainsκ\kappadepending only onTTand the HHS parameters. The general case follows from the caseλ=1\lambda=1by the exact same argument as is used in the proof of[BHMS24b, Thm. 2.9]; in fact, the latter theorem itself implies the present theorem as soon as we observe that the proof works for anyTTchosen sufficiently large in terms ofλ\lambdaand the “threshold”T0T_{0}from theλ=1\lambda=1case.
∎

The interested reader can refer to[CRHK24, Rem. 12.10], which is about an alternate proof due to Durham that also yields uniform constants.

## A.2.Cubical approximation theorem

Next, we recall the cubical approximation theorem from[BHS21], of which there are several variants with different proofs, for instance in[DMS23,Dur23,DZ22,CRHK24]. The following version is the same as the statements from[BHS21]and[CRHK24], except it is explicit about what the constants depend on.

## Proposition\thethm(Cubical approximation).

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be a hierarchically hyperbolic space of complexityχ\chi. Then there existsM0M_{0}, depending on the HHS parameters only, such that for allk∈ℕk\in\mathbb{N}there existsCC, depending only on the HHS parameters andkk, such that the following holds.

LetM1≔100​M0​C​kM_{1}\coloneqq 100M_{0}Ck. Then for allM≥M1M\geq M_{1}, there is a constantQ≥1Q\geq 1, depending onM,kM,k, and the HHS parameters only, satisfying the following.

LetA={x1,…,xk}⊂XA=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}\subset Xand let𝔘\mathfrak{U}be the set ofU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}such thatdU​(xi,xj)≥Md_{U}(x_{i},x_{j})\geq Mfor somei,ji,j. Then there exists a CAT(0) cube complex□A\Box_{A}and a mapf:□A→Xf:\Box_{A}\to Xsuch that:
- •

ffis a(Q,Q)(Q,Q)–quasi-isometric embedding anddH​a​u​s​(f​(□A),Hθ​(A))≤Qd_{Haus}(f(\Box_{A}),H_{\theta}(A))\leq Q.
- •

There existx^i1,…,x^is∈□A\hat{x}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\hat{x}_{i_{s}}\in\Box_{A}such thatdX​(f​(x^ij),xj)≤Cd_{X}(f(\hat{x}_{i_{j}}),x_{j})\leq Cfor alljjand□A\Box_{A}is equal to the convex hull in□A\Box_{A}of{x^i1,…,x^is}\{\hat{x}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\hat{x}_{i_{s}}\}. In particular,□A\Box_{A}has finitely many cubes.
- •

The mapffisQQ–quasimedian.
- •

Each hyperplanehhof□A{\Box_{A}}is labelled by an element of𝔘⊂𝔖\mathfrak{U}\subset\mathfrak{S}, and hyperplanesh,h′h,h^{\prime}cross if and only if their labelsU,U′U,U^{\prime}satisfyU⊥U′U\bot U^{\prime}. In particular,dim⁡□A≤χ\operatorname{dim}{\Box_{A}}\leq\chi.

Finally, ifγ\gammais a combinatorial geodesic in□A{\Box_{A}}, thenf∘γf\circ\gammais a(D,D)(D,D)–hierarchy path inXX, whereDDdepends only onM,kM,k, and the HHS parameters.

## Proof.

The only difference between this proposition and[CRHK24, Prop. 18.1]is that the current proposition says explicitly what the constantsM0M_{0}andQQdepend on. As noted in[CRHK24, Sec. 18], everything in the statement (except for the claim about the dependency of constants) is given by[BHS21, Thm. 2.1], except for the final assertion about hierarchy paths. Therefore, we will first explain why the proof of[BHS21, Thm. 2.1]gives effective constants, i.e., whyM0M_{0}can be chosen in terms of the HHS parameters, andQQcan be chosen in terms ofkkand the HHS parameters. Then we will verify the claim about hierarchy paths.


Constants in the proof from[BHS21].Now we trace through the proof of[BHS21, Thm. 2.1], starting with the constructions in[BHS21, Sec. 2]before the theorem:
- (1)

The finite treesTUT_{U}are(1,C)(1,C)–quasi-isometrically embedded in𝒞​U\mathcal{C}U, whereC≥1C\geq 1is a constant depending onEEandkk.
- (2)

The constantMMin[BHS21]is what we have here calledM0​CM_{0}C. In[BHS21], the set𝔘\mathfrak{U}is chosen to consist of thoseU∈𝔖U\in\mathfrak{S}for whichπU​(A)\pi_{U}(A)has diameter at least100​M0​C​k100M_{0}Ck, in our notation. So, once we have chosenM0M_{0}, the constantM1M_{1}from our current statement is determined. The constantM0M_{0}is chosen by imposing various (finitely many) constraints as the proof proceeds, all in terms of the HHS parameters only; we will mention these as they arise. The first is just thatM0>EM_{0}>E, and henceM>EM>E.
- (3)

The cardinality of𝔘\mathfrak{U}is bounded in terms ofdiamX⁡(A)\operatorname{\textup{{diam}}}_{X}(A)and the HHS parameters only (includingχ\chi), using the large link axiom, although what’s needed is just that𝔘\mathfrak{U}is finite. (See the remark on[BHS21, p. 947], or[CRHK24, Lem. 13.5].)
- (4)

The only constraints on constants arising in the construction of the walls inHθ​(A)H_{\theta}(A)(and hence the dual cube complex□A\Box_{A}, which is called𝒴\mathcal{Y}in[BHS21]) is thatM0≥EM_{0}\geq E.
- (5)

In Section 2.2 of[BHS21], there are three lemmas, Lemmas 2.3–2.5, whose hypotheses require that (in our notation)M0≥10​EM_{0}\geq 10E, which is the most restrictive hypothesis in any of these lemmas.
- (6)

Lemma 2.6 introduces a constantτ=τ​(M,k)\tau=\tau(M,k). The proof of this lemma makes it explicit that it is sufficient to chooseτ=50​M​k​(k−2)\tau=50Mk(k-2).
- (7)

Lemma 2.7 introduces a constantη\eta; the statement says that this depends only onM,kM,k, and the HHSXX, but the dependency onXXis only via the HHS parameters; specifically, the proof shows thatη=100​(M​k+C+2​E)\eta=100(Mk+C+2E)works.
- (8)

In the proof of Theorem 2.1 itself, the choice ofη\etadetermines another constantξ\xi, which depends only onη\etaand the HHS parameters; specifically,ξ=η​r0\xi=\eta r_{0}, wherer0r_{0}is the realisation theorem constant (Theorem2.4) discussed above.
- (9)

Lemma 2.8 does not influence the constants, and the constantNNfrom Lemma 2.9 depends only on an HHS parameter, the complexity, via Ramsey’s theorem.
- (10)

Lemma 2.10 provides a constantTTdepending onM,k,ξ,τM,k,\xi,\tauand the HHS(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}). The proof of Lemma 2.10 again makes it clear that the dependence on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})is only via dependence on the HHS parameters (not on the specific choice of spaceXXetc.). This uses[BHS21, Lem. 1.6], the “passing up large projections” lemma, in which the outputN0N_{0}depends on the input and on the HHS parameters (in particular, the constants in the large link axiom and the complexity) only.
- (11)

Lemma 2.12 does not involve constants. The proof of Lemma 2.11 makes clear that the constant implicit in the word “coarsely” in the statement depends onEEandθ\thetaand hence only on the HHS parameters.
- (12)

Lemma 2.13 provides a quasimedian constant. One must read the proof to check that this depends only on the HHS parameters andkk, but the key facts are that the constantsτ\tauandη\etadepend only on those things, and that the distance bounds involved in the construction of the coarse median mapμ\mu(see Section2.5above) also depend only on the HHS parameters.
- (13)

Finally, we examine the proof of Theorem 2.1 itself. The constants listed just above appear in that proof, and are bounded in terms of the HHS parameters andM,kM,k; hence the same is true of any quantity computed from those constants via some procedure that only depends on the HHS parameters. In particular, the constantC1′C_{1}^{\prime}in the proof of Theorem 2.1 is obtained by evaluating the uniqueness function, and the hierarchical quasiconvexity functionhh, on uniform inputs, and is thus uniform.

The constantC1′′C_{1}^{\prime\prime}is a distance formula quasi-isometry constant, with uniform threshold (depending onEEandMM), which is therefore uniform (see TheoremA.1). The constantC1′′′C_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}depends in a uniform way on the output of Lemma 2.6, i.e. onτ\tau, and is therefore uniform. Finally, the constantC1′′′′C_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}is the uniform constant from Lemma 2.13.

This establishes thatM0M_{0}depends only on the HHS parameters,CCdepends only on the HHS parameters andkk, andQQdepends on the HHS parameters,kk, andMM.

It just remains to prove the statement about hierarchy paths. As noted in[CRHK24, Rem. 18.2], this follows from the fact thatffis a(Q,Q)(Q,Q)–quasimedian quasi-isometric embedding together with Proposition2.6. ThatDDdepends only onM,kM,k, and the HHS parameters follows from the latter proposition and the fact, established above, thatQQis uniform.
∎

PropositionA.2immediately yields the following (which is also proven in[BHS19, Sec. 7]without using cubical approximation):{cor}

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS. Letμ:X3→2X\mu:X^{3}\to 2^{X}be the coarse map from Construction2.5. Thenμ\muis a coarse median in the sense of Definition2.5, and the coarse median parameters depend only on the HHS parameters.

## A.3.Non-canonical cone-off

The following proposition is almost exactly the same as Proposition 19.1 in[CRHK24]except for the clause about HHS parameters.

## Proposition\thethm(Equivariant cone-off, with parameters).

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS, letA≤Isom⁡(X)A\leq\operatorname{Isom}(X)be a group of HHS automorphisms, and let𝔘⊆𝔖\mathfrak{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}be anAA–invariant downward-closed set. Let(X^,d^)(\widehat{X},\widehat{d})be the non-canonical cone-off from Definition2.10. Then the pair(X^,𝔖−𝔘)(\widehat{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U})is a hierarchically hyperbolic space, whose HHS parameters are bounded above in terms of the HHS parameters of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})only. Moreover, theAA–action on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})induces an action ofAAon(X^,𝔖−𝔘)(\widehat{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U})by HHS automorphisms for which theAA–action on(X,d^)(X,\widehat{d})is an action by(C,C)(C,C)–quasi-isometries, whereCCdepends only on the HHS parameters of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}).

## Proof.

This is[CRHK24, Prop. 19.1](in turn a variant of[BHS17b, Prop. 2.4]) except we need to justify the statements about the HHS parameters of(X^,𝔖−𝔘)(\widehat{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U})andCC. As noted in the proof of[CRHK24, Prop. 19.1], theAA–action is by quasi-isometries, by the distance formula for the HHS(X^,𝔖−𝔘)(\widehat{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U}). HenceCCdepends only on the HHS parameters of(X^,𝔖−𝔘)(\widehat{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U}), so it suffices to prove the latter just depend on the HHS parameters for(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}).

This follows by examining the proof that(X^,𝔖−𝔘)(\widehat{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U})is in HHS. In[BHS17b, Sec. 2], the proof of Proposition 2.4 shows that all of the “new” HHS parameters except for the uniqueness function are bounded in terms of the “old” ones by a straightforward observation. For example, the underlying set ofX^\widehat{X}is justXX, and forU∈𝔖−𝔘U\in\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U}, the projectionX→𝒞​UX\to\mathcal{C}Uis the same coarse mapπU\pi_{U}as in the original HHS structure. So the consistency, bounded geodesic image, large links, and partial realisation axioms, which do not mention the metric onXX, hold unchanged for the new HHS structure. The same is true of the axioms that just concern the relations⊑,⊥,⋔\sqsubseteq,\bot,\pitchforkon𝔖\mathfrak{S}(such as finite complexity). The first place where the difference betweenddandd^\widehat{d}matters is in the proof thatπU:X^→𝒞​U\pi_{U}:\widehat{X}\to\mathcal{C}Uis coarsely lipschitz, but the constant is computed in[BHS17b]in terms of the HHS parameters of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}).

The remaining, and most complicated, thing to check is that the uniqueness function for(X^,𝔖−𝔘)(\widehat{X},\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U})is bounded above in terms of the HHS parameters for(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}). Here we follow the proof in[CRHK24, Proposition 19.1], which uses PropositionA.2and yields explicit estimates. Tracing through that proof, the inputs are:
- •

The uniqueness functionθu\theta_{u}for(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}).
- •

The constantθ\theta, which depends only on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}), given by[BHS19, Prop. 6.15].
- •

The HHS constantEE.
- •

ConstantsM0,M1,MM_{0},M_{1},Mthat depend only on the HHS parameters of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})and are provided by PropositionA.2, withk=2k=2.
- •

A constantN=N​(M)N=N(M)which depends only onMMand the HHS parameters, via[CRHK24, Proposition 13.1], essentially a consequence of the large link and finite complexity axioms for(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}).

In the remainder of the proof, we are given an arbitraryκ≥0\kappa\geq 0and pointsx,y∈X^x,y\in\widehat{X}withdU​(x,y)≤κd_{U}(x,y)\leq\kappafor allU∈𝔖−𝔘U\in\mathfrak{S}-\mathfrak{U}. The goal is to boundd^​(x,y)\widehat{d}(x,y)in terms ofκ\kappaand the HHS parameters of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}). First, Claim 12 in the proof of[CRHK24, Proposition 19.1]produces a constantν\nuthat depends only onκ\kappaand the HHS parameters of(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S}). Next, the constantNNin Claim 13 is the one mentioned above. In the final part of the proof, induction on the complexity of the original HHS yields a constantν1\nu^{1}, explicitly stated to depend only on the original HHS parameters andκ\kappa, and the estimated^​(x,y)≤N​(ν+ν1)\widehat{d}(x,y)\leq N(\nu+\nu^{1}). This shows that the new uniqueness function is bounded in terms of the old HHS parameters, as required.
∎

## A.4.Coarse injectivity

We recall the following theorem from[HHP23, Theorem A]:{thm}

Let(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})be an HHS. There is a metricσ\sigmaonXXand constantsM1,M2M_{1},M_{2}such that(X,σ)(X,\sigma)isM1M_{1}–coarsely injective andM2M_{2}–quasi-isometric to(X,d)(X,d), and any subgroupH≤Isom⁡(X,d)H\leq\operatorname{Isom}(X,d)acting by HHS automorphisms on(X,𝔖)(X,\mathfrak{S})acts by isometries on(X,σ)(X,\sigma). Moreover the constantsM1,M2M_{1},M_{2}depend only on the HHS parameters.

## Proof.

Using Lemma2.12, we can assume thatXXis a graph with the usual combinatorial metric. The theorem is now exactly the content of[HHP23, Theorem A]; we just need to go through their proof and check that the constants depend only on the HHS parameters.

The definition ofσ\sigmarequires an arbitrary choice of positive constant; we choose 1.
- (1)

By PropositionA.2, there existsκ\kappa, depending only on the HHS parameters, such that: for any pair of pointsx,y∈Xx,y\in X, there exists a finite CAT(0) cube complexQQwith dimension at mostχ\chiand aκ\kappa–quasimedian(κ,κ)(\kappa,\kappa)–quasi-isometryλ:Q→Hθ​(x,y)\lambda:Q\rightarrow H_{\theta}(x,y), withθ\thetaas in Lemma2.8. There existsκ′≥1\kappa^{\prime}\geq 1, depending only onκ\kappaandhθ​(0)h_{\theta}(0), such thatλ\lambdahas a quasi-inverse which is a(κ′,κ′)(\kappa^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})–quasi-isometry and isκ′\kappa^{\prime}–quasi-median.
- (2)

By[NWZ19, Lemmas 2.18 and 2.19], there existsH5≥0H_{5}\geq 0, which depends only the coarse median parameters ofXX, and hence, by CorollaryA.2, only depends on the HHS parameters, such that:d​(μ​(a,b,μ​(x,y,z)),μ​(μ​(a,b,x),μ​(a,b,y),z))≤H5,\displaystyle d(\mu(a,b,\mu(x,y,z)),\mu(\mu(a,b,x),\mu(a,b,y),z))\leq H_{5},d​(μ​(a,b,μ​(x,y,z)),μ​(a,b,x),μ​(a,b,y),μ​(a,b,z))≤H5\displaystyle d(\mu(a,b,\mu(x,y,z)),\mu(a,b,x),\mu(a,b,y),\mu(a,b,z))\leq H_{5}

for alla,b,x,y,z∈Xa,b,x,y,z\in X.
- (3)

LetL≔max⁡{1+h​(0),2+H5}L\coloneqq\max\{1+h(0),2+H_{5}\}. Then, according to the proof of[HHP23, Proposition 2.16], we can takeM2=χ​κ′2​LM_{2}=\chi{\kappa^{\prime}}^{2}L.
- (4)

By[HHP23, Lemma 2.22], there existsε≥0\varepsilon\geq 0such that, for anyx,y,z∈Xx,y,z\in Xsuch thatx=μ​(x′,y,z)x=\mu(x^{\prime},y,z)for somex′∈Xx^{\prime}\in X, we haved​(x,μ​(x,y,z))≤εd(x,\mu(x,y,z))\leq\varepsilon.
One can chooseε≔r0′+r0+h​(0)​(r0′+1)\varepsilon\coloneqq r_{0}^{\prime}+r_{0}+h(0)(r_{0}^{\prime}+1), wherer0,r0′r_{0},r_{0}^{\prime}are constants provided by[Bow19, Lemma 8.1(2)]which only depend on the coarse median parameters ofXX, which in turn only depend on the HHS parameters, again by CorollaryA.2.
- (5)

LetCσ′≔64​(1+κ)​χ+4​M2​(1+κ)+4​κ+4+2​(4​h​(0)+4).C_{\sigma}^{\prime}\coloneqq 64(1+\kappa)\chi+4M_{2}(1+\kappa)+4\kappa+4+2(4h(0)+4).

Then(X,σ)(X,\sigma)isCσ′C_{\sigma}^{\prime}–weakly roughly geodesic[HHP23, Prop. 2.21].
- (6)

ForM3≔ε+2+3​Cσ′M_{3}\coloneqq\varepsilon+2+3C_{\sigma}^{\prime}, balls in(X,σ)(X,\sigma)areM3M_{3}–median quasiconvex[HHP23, Lem. 2.23].
- (7)

There existsk:[0,∞)→[0,∞)k:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty), depending only onM3M_{3}and the HHS parameters, such that balls in(X,σ)(X,\sigma)arekk–hierarchically quasiconvex[RST23, Prop. 5.11].
- (8)

For allr≥0r\geq 0, there existsR​(r)≥0R(r)\geq 0, depending only onrrandEE, such that the following holds. If𝒬\mathcal{Q}is a collection of boundedkk–hierarchically quasiconvex subspaces ofXXwhich arerr–close (with respect todd), then there is a pointx∈Xx\in Xwithd​(x,Q)≤R​(r)d(x,Q)\leq R(r)for eachQ∈𝒬Q\in\mathcal{Q}[HHP23, Theorem 3.5].
- (9)

Following the proof of[HHP23, Corollary 3.6], let us check that(X,σ)(X,\sigma)is coarsely injective with constants only depending on the HHS parameters. Let{Bσ​(xi,ri):i∈I}\{B_{\sigma}(x_{i},r_{i}):i\in I\}be a family of balls in(X,σ)(X,\sigma)such thatσ​(xi,xj)≤ri+rj\sigma(x_{i},x_{j})\leq r_{i}+r_{j}for alli,j∈Ii,j\in I. Since(X,σ)(X,\sigma)is weakly roughly geodesic with constantCσ′C_{\sigma}^{\prime}, the balls{Bσ​(xi,ri+2​Cσ′):i∈I}\{B_{\sigma}(x_{i},r_{i}+2C_{\sigma}^{\prime}):i\in I\}intersect pairwise. For eachi∈Ii\in I, letBiB_{i}be the image ofBσ​(xi,ri+2​Cσ′)B_{\sigma}(x_{i},r_{i}+2C_{\sigma}^{\prime})under the identityM2M_{2}–quasi-isometry(X,σ)→(X,d)(X,\sigma)\rightarrow(X,d). EachBiB_{i}isM3M_{3}–coarsely median convex and thereforekk–hierarchically quasiconvex. Thus there is a pointx∈Xx\in Xsuch thatd​(x,Bi)≤R​(0)d(x,B_{i})\leq R(0)for eachi∈Ii\in I. So(X,σ)(X,\sigma)is coarsely injective with constantM1≔M2​(R​(0)+Cσ′)+M2M_{1}\coloneqq M_{2}(R(0)+C_{\sigma}^{\prime})+M_{2}. ∎
